The chain handler feeds the block-accepted, block-connected, block-disconnected, and reorg-bracket events straight to the generator's channel, and the mempool gained dcrd's `OnVoteReceived` hook (an optional `VoteReceiver` the accepted-vote path notifies, pinned by a native test replaying the vote battery).  `getwork` serves over the live generator through the ported handler — the current-template read, the subscribe-and-wait for a fresh template, and the block-time update — with the chain gaining dcrd's exported `IsBlake3PowAgendaActive` for the work-data serialization, and `regentemplate` forces a rebuild.  Pinned by an end-to-end check that starts the generator over a live regnet battery chain, waits for the startup template, and serves a `getwork` request with real work data and target, plus a force-regen check.  The CPU miner's discrete `generate` path now runs in the daemon (dcrd `internal/mining/cpuminer`): the proof-of-work solve loop is ported as a pure, `no_std`, chain-free core in `dcroxide-mining` (`solveBlock` — the extra-nonce/nonce/timestamp search with dcrd's exact `nonce%65535` tick cadence and the break-at-`maxNonce` placement that keeps the u32 nonce from wrapping back into the range, the agenda-gated BLAKE-256/BLAKE3 proof-of-work hash over the header serialized once and patched in place at dcrd's timestamp, nonce and extra-nonce offsets (136, 140, 144), with the nonce and extra nonce written back to the header only on a solution, the target decoded once with dcrd's negative-bits and overflow guards and compared as a fixed-width big-endian value (dcrd's uint256 `LtEq`) rather than a per-hash big integer, and the injected extra-nonce offset, block-time updater, cancellation predicate, and clock so the core owns no chain or clock), and the node crate drives it: `NodeCpuMiner` implements the RPC `CPUMiner` seam and its `generate_n_blocks` runs dcrd's `GenerateNBlocks` state machine synchronously on the RPC thread — subscribing to the background generator's template stream, solving each fresh template on a cancellable worker thread, submitting a solution through the piece-152 `process_block` seam (so a mined block validates, connects, relays, and prunes the mempool exactly like a network block), skipping a re-delivered template by its block hash (dcrd's `discretePrevTemplate` pointer identity), and returning the hashes that extended the chain; `getmininginfo`/`getgenerate`/`gethashespersec` answer the idle worker count (dcrd's `defaultNumWorkers`) and zero hash rate, and `setgenerate` records the worker count with dcrd's clamping and `setgenerate true` now starts the continuous background miner (dcrd's `Run` + `speedMonitor` + `miningWorkerController` + `generateBlocks` + `solver` goroutine choreography, translated to OS threads): the daemon-held `MinerRuntime` owns a speed-monitor thread and a worker-controller thread (started idle when a template generator runs, so continuous mining works under `--norpc`, exactly as dcrd runs `go s.cpuMiner.Run` unconditionally), `setgenerate`/`--generate` store the worker count on a shared atomic and poke the controller, which launches or stops `generateBlocks` worker threads to match — each subscribing to the generator, solving the current template on a cancellable solver thread (the connection wait when peers are required, the connectionless per-parent `maxSimnetToMine` cap, and the submit-failure retry all ported), and submitting through the block-submit seam; `gethashespersec`/`getmininginfo` read the live rate the speed monitor sums from the per-worker statistics over a request/reply channel (dcrd's `queryHashesPerSec` rendezvous), and the now-reachable `normalMining` guard refuses a discrete `generate` while continuous mining runs.  The channel-select of the template subscription against the `notifyBlocks` block feed becomes a watch thread (`cpuminer::DiscreteWatch`).  `std::sync::mpsc` cannot select between the two, so the watch polls the chain tip (a try-lock every 10 ms), the `generate 0` flag and the miner's quit, and wakes the template wait.  A discrete run therefore ends within one poll interval of the block that reaches its target, whatever its source, rather than on the connect notification itself (`tests/review_generate_return.rs`).  Pinned by solve-core unit tests (a real proof of work found and verified under both hash functions, the cancel and 65535-tick cadence) and an end-to-end regnet check that `generate 1` and `generate 3` mine real blocks that become the main-chain tip.  The RPC control and chain-query surface grows four more seams over already-live subsystems: `getcfilterv2` answers the committed filter and header-commitment proof from `Chain::filter_by_block_hash` (the very source the peer `getcfilterv2` already serves, with `ErrNoFilter` mapped to the block-not-found result), `verifychain` runs the ported `check_block_sanity` over each in-range block (dcrd's `rpcSanityChecker`, with the daemon wall clock standing in for the chain's median time exactly as its own `process_block` path does), `getnetworkinfo` reports the address manager's local addresses and the three per-network reachability descriptions the config's `parse_network_interfaces` already derived (dcrd's `generateNetworkInfo`), and `stop` fires the daemon's graceful shutdown by setting the shared interrupt and sending on the very channel SIGINT does (dcrd's non-blocking `requestProcessShutdown` send), so the node actually stops rather than reporting success and staying up; an end-to-end regnet check over the live listener pins all four (the committed filter returned byte-for-byte, the block-not-found mapping, `verifychain 1 1` true, `getnetworkinfo`'s local address and networks, and `stop` both answering `dcrd stopping.` and firing the shutdown hook).  `getpeerinfo` now answers over the live peer set (dcrd's `rpcConnManager.ConnectedPeers` over `peerState`): the handshake hands the shared `Arc<Mutex<Peer>>` to the server, which registers it (with the connection's local address) in the sync-manager peer registry keyed by the peer's own id (assigned from the process-wide counter as its version message is read, dcrd `readRemoteVersionMsg`'s `nodeCount`, so a peer rejected after that read still uses one up), and the connection-manager seam snapshots each live peer into the ported `getpeerinfo` result — folding the peer's nanosecond send/receive/connect times to unix seconds, reading `relaytxes` from the relay facts, and drawing the id from the registry key, which is that same peer id (dcrd's `ID: statsSnap.ID`) and the space `sync_peer_id` marks as the sync node; `version` is the peer's advertised protocol version (dcrd's `StatsSnapshot.Version`), not the negotiated one.  The snapshot is lock-order safe (the registry lock is dropped before any per-peer lock, never nesting the map inside a peer lock) and poison tolerant (a peer whose input thread panicked and poisoned its mutex is skipped rather than propagating the panic to every `getpeerinfo` call).  `banscore` reports the live decaying score (dcrd's `banScore.Int()`): the abuse-control handlers and the peer registry share the per-peer `DynamicBanScore`, exactly the object dcrd's RPC adaptor reads off the serverPeer.  `bytessent`/`bytesrecv`/`lastsend`/`lastrecv` report live per-peer accounting: the serving loops attribute each message's wire bytes and timestamp to the peer (dcrd bumping the peer's counters on every read and write), with the version handshake's traffic folded in exactly as dcrd's counted negotiation, alongside the server-wide `getnettotals` totals.  Those server-wide totals count every byte a read or write moved, including those of a message that fails to read (a bad checksum, an unknown command, a payload cut short) and of a write that fails partway, because dcrd's `readMessage`/`writeMessage` pass `ReadMessageN`'s and `WriteMessageN`'s byte counts to `OnRead`/`OnWrite`, which add them to the server totals before the error is looked at.  The per-peer `bytesrecv`/`bytessent` count only messages that were read or written in full, whereas dcrd's `readMessage`/`writeMessage` also add a failed message's bytes to the peer's own counters.  Only a `getpeerinfo` racing the disconnect could see that difference: every read or write error ends the connection in both implementations (dcrd's `inHandler` and `outHandler` call `Disconnect` straight after), and dcrd's `ConnectedPeers` skips a peer once it is disconnecting.  The manual peer-control RPCs begin to answer over the live daemon: a `permanent` flag and the remote address ride the same `SyncPeers` registry entry (recorded post-handshake, dropped on disconnect — dcrd's `persistentPeers` lifetime), so `getaddednodeinfo` lists the persistent peers (always connected outbound nodes, matching dcrd's `PersistentPeers`), `node disconnect` shuts a non-permanent peer's socket while treating a permanent one as "peer not found" so the handler emits dcrd's "use remove" hint, and `getaddednodeinfo`'s DNS detail resolves through the configured `dcrdLookup` routing (Tor resolution under --proxy, the onion route for `.onion` hosts).  The connect/remove half of the cluster (`addnode`, `node connect/remove`) runs over a request/reply command channel into the outbound dial driver, covered below.  The block templater's long-poll blocking-during-reorg refinement remains.  The block-submit seam now runs in the daemon (dcrd rpcSyncMgr's `SubmitBlock` handing a locally-solved block to `syncManager.ProcessBlock`): `submitblock` and the getwork solution branch — both already faithfully ported — funnel through `NodeRpcSyncManager::submit_block`, which locks the shared sync manager and calls the netsync `SyncManager::process_block` (the exact port of dcrd's `ProcessBlock`, driving the block through the same validate/connect/relay/prune path as a network block, so a locally-mined block relays and maintains the mempool exactly like a synced one) and maps the netsync `ProcessBlockFailure` to the RPC `SubmitBlockFailure`; every failure classifies as a rule error because the ported `Chain::process_block` only ever surfaces `RuleError`s (database and assertion faults panic per the decision-core convention) and dcrd's `ErrDuplicateBlock` is itself a `blockchain.RuleError`, so `submitblock` answers `null` on acceptance and `rejected: <rule error>` on rejection while getwork answers `true`/`false`, dcrd's non-rule internal-error branch being unreachable and documented as such.  The getwork handler's mining gate is un-panicked in the same piece: the live daemon config gained an idle CPU-miner stand-in (`is_mining()` false, dcrd's default-off miner) so `getwork` — which reads `is_mining` on its very first line — serves work and accepts solutions; the generating miner behind `generate`/`setgenerate` followed, covered below.  Pinned by a seam test that submits a battery block through `submit_block` (accepted, extending the tip) then rejects its resubmission as dcrd's "already have block" duplicate rule error, and an end-to-end HTTP check that `submitblock` over the live listener answers `null` on acceptance (the chain advancing to the submitted block) and `rejected: already have block` on the duplicate.  The fee estimator now runs in the daemon (dcrd `newServer` always building the estimator and handing it to both the mempool and the RPC server, with no CLI gate): one shared `Arc<Mutex<Estimator>>` is built over the network's minimum relay fee (dcrd's `EstimatorConfig` — buckets from the relay floor up to a hundred times it plus the extra wallet-compat bucket), installed on the pool through a new `FeeEstimatorSink` mempool hook (dcrd's `AddTxToFeeEstimation`/`RemoveTxFromFeeEstimation` config closures) that fires the add on every accepted mempool transaction with dcrd's `(hash, fee, int64(SerializeSize()), txType)` at the tail of `addTransaction` beside the exists-addr hook and the remove on every eviction inside `removeTransaction`, and read by `estimatesmartfee` through the RPC `FeeEstimator` seam; the chain handler drives it exactly as dcrd's notification cases do — the first accepted block enables it at that height only once the sync is current (`if !s.feeEstimator.IsEnabled() { Enable(block.Height()) }` sits at the end of dcrd's NTBlockAccepted case, guarded by the `!AllowUnsyncedMining && !IsCurrent() { return }` early-return at the top, so pre-sync mempool contents never skew the estimate; the port applies that gate at the same drain that relays the accepted announcement — `drain_pending_accepted_announcements`, where the enable is the last step under the identical is-current gate — because the callback runs under the chain mutex and cannot re-check is-current inline) and every connected block's regular and stake transaction hashes feed `ProcessBlock` before the mempool removal loop (dcrd's documented ordering, "because the mempool will alert the estimator of the txs that are leaving", with the coinbase and treasurybase ignored because they were never in the pool, and Enable-at-H-then-ProcessBlock-at-H early-returning for the enable block exactly as dcrd's `block_height <= best_height` guard does); the estimator starts empty each run because dcrd's leveldb bucket persistence is deferred (a fresh dcrd database re-primes identically over the first post-sync blocks), and `estimatefee` still echoes the minimum relay fee dcrd-faithfully.  `EstimateFeeError` gained dcrd's exact `Display`/`Error` strings so `estimatesmartfee` surfaces "not enough transactions seen for estimation" as an internal error (dcrd's `-32603`) over an unprimed estimator and rejects a non-conservative mode with dcrd's invalid-parameter error (`-8`) before consulting it.  Pinned by seam unit tests (the mempool sink recording and clearing a transaction, the block feed shifting a tracked transaction out of the mempool statistics into the mined counters, and the shared-estimator `Send` bound) and the end-to-end HTTP check that `estimatesmartfee` over the wired but unprimed estimator answers dcrd's estimation error and rejects the bad mode.  An adversarial review pass shaped the wiring: the signal handler arms at the top of `run()` before the database work so an interrupt aborts the index drops and catch-up gracefully (dcrd installs its shutdown listener before `loadBlockDB`), the RPC block runs before the peer-to-peer listeners so the notification callback is installed before any peer can connect a block (dcrd registers the handler at chain construction — a block landing in that window would otherwise desync the expected-height state machine and fail every later index update until the heights line up again), and every failed index update logs its error under INDX (dcrd's `log.Error(err)` in `handleIndexUpdates`, which keeps consuming notifications after its `s.cancel()`), so an index that fell out of step reports the missing-notification error for each later block instead of going silent.  Noted liveness divergence: the ported handlers all run under the one RPC server mutex, so `getrawtransaction`'s three-second sync wait stalls every concurrent RPC and websocket delivery for its duration where dcrd's per-request goroutines delay only the one caller; the wait only triggers while the index tip trails the chain tip by five blocks or fewer, a window the synchronous drain keeps to the inside of a processing call in practice — lifting it needs per-connection handler concurrency, deferred with that redesign.  The rotating file-logging backend remains a stub (output goes to the standard streams), and the network-interface and cross-user home resolution used by a small part of the configuration pipeline are not yet wired.  Pinned by command line integration checks over the built binary (the version, help, debug-level show, and unknown-flag exits, plus a fresh-database `--nolisten` startup that opens the block database and loads the genesis chain state at height zero, and a `--listen=127.0.0.1:0` startup that announces it is serving peers on the bound ephemeral port), and by end-to-end dispatch checks over a genesis chain served through the full runtime (a negotiated peer's getheaders answered with the empty low-work headers message, a getdata served the genesis block from the chain with misses consolidated into notfound, an empty getdata request dropping the connection through the intake gate, the genesis committed filter served with its inclusion proof singly and batched, and the pre-stake-validation empty init state sent exactly once per connection).  Also ported (the threaded server runtime, translating `server.go`'s `Run`/`peerHandler` goroutines to OS threads and channels rather than an async runtime, per the project's concurrency decision): the listener backbone (`ListenerRuntime`) — it binds each parsed listener spec (expanding the wildcard host to the family-appropriate any-address for the `tcp4`/`tcp6` split, with `SO_REUSEADDR` set outside Windows on both families as Go's listeners set it, so a restart rebinds over the previous run's closing connections).  A spec that cannot bind is logged as dcrd's `initListeners` logs it (`Can't listen on <addr>: <err>`, SRVR warning) and skipped, and startup fails only when nothing binds, with `newServer`'s "no valid listen address", which the binary reports as `Unable to start server: ...`.  It accepts inbound connections on a dedicated non-blocking thread per listener, which announces itself with connmgr `listenHandler`'s `Server listening on <addr>` (CMGR info) and hands each accepted connection to a supplied handler, and coordinates a graceful shutdown by signalling those accept threads and joining them; the connection manager's outbound dialing and seeding, the peer version handshake, the per-peer input and output loops, the sync manager, and the RPC server plug into this same shutdown coordination, and the runtime is wired into the binary's `run` — all covered below.  Pinned by integration checks that bind ephemeral listeners, connect to the assigned ports, and confirm both single- and multi-listener inbound acceptance and a clean joined shutdown.  Also ported (the connection framing): `WireTransport` — the concrete `MsgTransport` the ported peer module's version handshake and per-peer message loops run over, framing whole wire messages over any byte stream (a TCP connection in the daemon, an in-memory stream in tests) with the ported wire codec — reading the fixed 24-byte header first and rejecting a header-declared payload length above the global cap before any payload allocation (so a hostile peer cannot force a huge read), and tallying the wire bytes moved in each direction for the peer loop's byte accounting; the idle read deadline dcrd sets before each read is applied by the peer loop on the underlying stream.  Pinned by unit checks (the framed bytes match the wire codec, an empty-payload and a payload-carrying message round-trip through an in-memory stream, and an oversized header length is rejected without reading a payload) and an integration check that round-trips messages both directions over a real loopback TCP connection.  Also ported (the peer environment and handshake): `NodePeerEnv` — the daemon's concrete `PeerEnv`, supplying the ported peer module's injected clock (the wall clock in unix nanoseconds), randomness (the process-wide `crypto/rand` generator for the version nonce, as dcrd's peer module draws it), and address shuffle (dcrd's `rand.ShuffleSlice` over that same generator) — together with the conversion from an accepted socket address into the wire network address a peer is associated with; with these a `Peer` runs the ported version negotiation straight over the `WireTransport`.  Pinned by unit checks of the environment (the clock is a recent positive time, the random source varies, the shuffle preserves the address multiset, and the socket conversion keeps the raw IP bytes) and an integration check where an inbound and an outbound peer complete the full version exchange over a real loopback TCP connection and each learns the other's advertised protocol version, services, and user agent.  Also ported (the per-peer input pump, `inHandler`): `classify_incoming` — the message-type dispatch as a decision core over the ported peer handlers (a second version or a second verack disconnects with dcrd's reason, a first verack marks the peer, a ping yields the matching pong reply, a pong records the ping round-trip, a sendheaders sets the wants-headers preference, and every other message is forwarded to the server handlers) with `send_verack` (dcrd `start`'s post-negotiation `QueueMessage(verack)`) and `run_peer_input`, the read loop that queues each immediate reply on the outbound queue and forwards each message until a read error (a closed connection or an idle read timeout) or a protocol disconnect.  dcrd's stall detector arrives later.  The read and write halves share the peer as a `&Mutex<Peer>` — the blocking read is taken without the lock held so the ping timer and the server make progress, and every write (the input pump's protocol replies included) goes through the outbound queue so all writes stay on the single output loop.  The keepalive is the ping schedule of `run_peer_timers`, the one timer thread a served peer runs for the keepalive and the stall check (`run_ping_timer` runs the ping schedule alone); every interval it draws a fresh nonce and queues a ping, until a shutdown signal or the queue closes (the ping ticker in dcrd's `outHandler`, `peer.go:1812-1814`).  The output loop stamps each ping as the outstanding one when it takes it off the queue to write it, whoever queued it (the keepalive or the `ping` RPC's broadcast), as dcrd's `outHandler` sets `lastPingNonce`/`lastPingTime` for every `MsgPing` (`peer.go:1776-1777`), so the answering pong is matched and its round trip excludes the time the ping spent queued.  Pinned by unit checks of the dispatch (ping-to-pong, the first-then-duplicate verack, the second-version disconnect, the sendheaders flag, and the pong clearing the outstanding ping); an integration check where the inbound peer, after the handshake, queues its verack, splits the socket, runs the output loop on its own thread, and runs the input loop over the shared peer — marking the remote's verack, answering a ping with the matching pong through the queue, and forwarding both messages in order — over a real loopback TCP connection, ending when the remote closes; a ping-timer check that the timer thread queues a keepalive ping and stops on the shutdown signal, and an output-loop check that a ping queued from anywhere is stamped when written (`a_ping_is_stamped_when_written_whoever_queued_it`).  Also ported (the per-peer output handler, `outHandler`): `OutboundQueue` — a cloneable queue handle (dcrd `QueueMessage`) the server, the input pump's replies, and the ping timer send through — and `run_peer_output`, the writer loop that drains the queue and writes each message to the connection until the queue is closed (a local shutdown) or a write fails, so all writes to a connection are serialized on one thread.  dcrd's separate inventory trickle queue (`QueueInventory`) and the send semaphore are later refinements; this is the plain message queue.  Pinned by an integration check that queues messages, drains them over a real loopback TCP connection, and confirms they arrive in the order queued and the loop shuts down cleanly when the queue closes, plus a check that queueing to a closed queue fails.  Also ported (the connection assembly): `run_peer_connection` — dcrd `peer.go`'s `start` plus the per-peer goroutine set as OS threads: it sets the read deadline to the idle timeout, splits the socket into read and write halves, runs the version handshake (inbound or outbound per the peer) over the read half before starting the loops — framing the handshake at the local maximum protocol version and then lowering both transports to the negotiated version, as dcrd re-reads the peer's protocol version per message — shares the negotiated peer as an `Arc<Mutex<Peer>>`, queues the verack, runs the output loop and the ping timer on their own threads, drives the input loop on the calling thread, and on disconnect shuts the socket down (so a write blocked against a peer that stopped reading unblocks, as dcrd's `Disconnect` closes the conn), signals the ping timer, closes the outbound queue, and joins both threads before returning why the connection stopped.  Pinned by an integration check where the server side runs the whole connection — accept, associate, handshake, and serve — while a remote peer negotiates, exchanges verack, and pings, receiving the pong, then closes: the connection forwards every message in order and stops with a read error, with the ping interval and idle timeout set long so neither fires.  Also ported (serving inbound peers): `inbound_peer_handler` — the listener runtime's accept handler (dcrd `server.inboundPeerConnected`): each accepted connection is handed to its own thread, which builds a fresh inbound peer from a `PeerTemplate` (network, advertised services, user agent with dcrd `newPeerConfig`'s pre-release comment `(pre)`, the idle and ping timeouts, the blocks-only `DisableRelayTx` flag, and the configured proxy), associates it with the remote address, and runs the whole connection; the server-handler dispatch (relay, inv, getdata) is a no-op until the peer-handler piece, so a served peer completes the handshake and is kept alive with ping and pong.  Each live connection is tracked in a `ConnectedPeers` registry (a socket handle per peer) so a shutdown can disconnect them all — shutting down each socket unblocks the peer's read loop so it winds down (the connected-peer half of dcrd's `peerState`); a drop guard deregisters a peer even when its serving thread unwinds from a panic, the registry's locks recover from poisoning so a crashed peer thread cannot break the shutdown sweep, and a transient `accept` error waits out a poll interval instead of killing the listener, logged as dcrd's `listenHandler` logs it (`Can't accept connection: <err>`, CMGR error, not during shutdown).  The would-block poll, EINTR and ECONNABORTED stay silent because Go's `Accept` retries those internally.  Accepted divergences: the error texts in `Can't listen on`/`Can't accept connection` are std's `io::Error` rendering ("Address already in use (os error 98)"), not Go's `*net.OpError` text ("listen tcp6 :9108: bind: address already in use").  A persistent accept error is retried every 50 ms, where dcrd retries at once, so the log gets at most 20 such lines a second.  Pinned by an integration check that starts a listener runtime with the serving handler, connects as an outbound peer, negotiates (learning the server's user agent), and confirms the server answers a ping with a pong before a clean shutdown; and a check that a served peer registers as live, then deregisters once `disconnect_all` tears its connection down.  The legacy mining-state exchange answers over the live chain (dcrd `OnGetMiningState`'s latch, its blank push that aborts on zero blocks so an early chain sends nothing, the vote-list truncation against the BLOCK limit kept bug for bug, and `OnMiningState` requesting the advertised blocks and votes through the sync manager), with the eligible parents drawn from the ported `SortParentsByVotes` — the same sorted, majority-vote-filtered list now also feeding getinitstate's head blocks.  The StakeShuffle mix messages now route through a shared pool: the daemon builds one `mixpool.Pool` over a chain adapter (`NodeMixChain` for the tip and params, `NodeMixUtxoFetcher` validating pair-request ownership against the live UTXO set through `Chain::fetch_utxo_entry`, exactly as dcrd's mixpool `utxoFetcher` over `FetchUtxoEntry`) and shares it between the getdata MIX serve path and the netsync manager (dcrd's one `s.mixMsgPool`); the eight `OnMix*` handlers ignore mix traffic entirely under --blocksonly (dcrd's `onMixMessage` blocksonly gate), mark each received message known to the sending peer before processing (dcrd's `sp.AddKnownInventory`, so the accept-time relay never echoes it back to the sender), and hand the wire mix message to that pool with the sync-manager lock held only for the rejected-message and request bookkeeping on either side of the acceptance, never across it (dcrd's `OnMixMsg` → `SyncManager.OnMixMsg` → `mixpool.AcceptMessage`, which takes only `requestMtx` around that bookkeeping; transaction intake has the same shape around `ProcessTransaction`), announcing every accepted message — the delivered one plus any orphan its acceptance un-orphaned — to the peers as MIX inventory and to the subscribed websocket clients (dcrd's `AnnounceMixMessages` → `relayMixMessages` + `s.rpcServer.NotifyMixMessages`, the latter a no-op under --norpc), requesting a referenced-but-missing pair request back from the sender on the orphan path (dcrd's `MissingOwnPR` → `RequestMixMsgFromPeer`), and disconnecting a peer whose message is a rule violation bannable for its advertised services; the getdata MIX serve path resolves each requested hash through the same pool's recent-message lookup (dcrd's `s.mixMsgPool.RecentMessage`, which also consults the LRU of messages just removed from the pool so a peer that advertised a now-confirmed message is still served it), queuing the served message in request order and missing a hash the pool never held into the consolidated notfound.  Pinned by a wire↔pool round-trip over all eight message variants (a mis-paired conversion arm surfaces as an inequality) and an end-to-end getdata check that an absent mix message misses into notfound.  The `addblock` and `promptsecret` tool commands are ported as workspace binaries.  `addblock` reads dcrd's bootstrap file format (`<network u32> <length u32> <serialized block>` records) and runs each block through the chain engine with bulk-import mode enabled (dcrd `EnableBulkImportMode`, skipping the script checks), creating the enabled transaction and exists-address indexes and catching them up before the import while leaving them untouched during it — dcrd's chain never notifies the index subscriber (only the daemon's server does, and addblock has no server), so its importer defers the imported blocks' indexing to the next daemon start's catch-up, and the port does the same — with dcrd's `loadConfig` gauntlet (network exclusivity, database type, per-network datadir namespacing, and infile existence with dcrd's exact error texts — including the quirk that `--help` exits nonzero because `realMain` treats the go-flags help error as a failure) riding the same go-flags scanner the daemon's pinned configuration pipeline uses (the scanner is parameterized over an option registry and value sink; the daemon's path is unchanged and its 52 syntax scenarios still pin it); the record reader keeps `readBlock`'s outcomes byte for byte (clean EOF only at the network field, Go's `unexpected EOF` on partial fields, the network-mismatch and payload-cap texts inside the `error reading from input file:` wrap), the importer keeps `processBlock`'s order (stats before the already-known skip, the does-not-link error printing the missing parent hash, the orphan and does-not-extend errors) and `logProgress`'s cadence (10ms-truncated durations, dcrd's pluralization, the per-record `lastHeight` counter, and the quirk that a zero interval logs every block despite the help text saying it disables announcements — the block time renders in pinned UTC where dcrd uses the host zone); dcrd's three importer goroutines run as one synchronous loop (only the read/process overlap is traded away) and its separate leveldb UTXO database has no equivalent in the port's single-database architecture.  Pinned by config/reader/importer unit tests, an end-to-end import of a battery prefix into a fresh regnet chain (the pre-created transaction index staying at its pre-import tip through the run and a fresh catch-up indexing the imported blocks, dcrd's deferred indexing) plus the already-known re-import and the gap/mismatch errors, and CLI checks over the built binary (the help/conflict/dbtype/infile exits and an empty-file mainnet import creating the database and reporting zero blocks).  `promptsecret` prompts on stderr, reads the secret with terminal echo disabled, writes it to stdout `-n` times, and zeroes the buffer, keeping Go's `flag` package exits (usage on `-h` with exit 0, parse errors with exit 2 and Go's normalized `parse error`/`value out of range` texts); the workspace forbids unsafe code, so the echo flip runs through `stty` on unix (failing on a non-terminal stdin exactly like Go's `ReadPassword` on a pipe) and reports echo control as unsupported on windows — a documented divergence.  Each `stty` call gets the tool's own stdin (`Command::output` would give it a null one, and the snapshot then failed on every terminal).  The secret is read a byte at a time and written through unbuffered duplicates of the standard descriptors, as Go's `os.Stdin`/`os.Stdout` reach them, so no std stream buffer keeps a copy after the zeroing.  The `gencerts` tool command is ported as a workspace binary over new certificate machinery in the certgen crate: dcrd's go-flags command line over the shared scanner (help exits 0, parse errors 1, a wrong argument count or unknown algorithm prints the help to stderr and exits 2 — with dcrd's `%q`-quoted unknown-algorithm text — `-C`/`-K` pairing, `-L` local-host appending, and the force checks with Go's stat-error-is-exists `fileExists`), key generation for the elliptic-curve and Ed25519 algorithms (P-256/P-384/P-521 by rejection sampling and Ed25519 — `p384` is a new RustCrypto dependency alongside the p256/p521 dependencies; dcrd's `RSA4096` is not offered, a documented divergence: the `rsa` crate carries the unfixed Marvin-attack advisory RUSTSEC-2023-0071 and no dcrd daemon component uses RSA), Go `newTemplate` (first-host-or-organization CN (left out of the subject when empty, and never a PrintableString when it holds `*` or `&`), Go `idna.ToASCII` (bare Punycode, case-preserving) hostnames split from parsed IPs into the SAN, a day of backdating, the 2049 UTCTime clamp, the already-elapsed refusal, 128-bit random serials, and the int64 nanosecond wrap of the years arithmetic), self-signed authorities and CA-issued certificates with Go `x509.CreateCertificate`'s behaviors (KeyUsage digitalSignature plus keyCertSign under `-S`, BasicConstraints with the CA boolean only for authorities, the SubjectKeyIdentifier (truncated SHA-256, Go 1.25's default) auto-computed for CA certificates only, the AuthorityKeyIdentifier copied from the parent only when the subject differs from the issuer, the SAN omitted without hosts, and per-curve signature hashes), PKCS#8 marshaling for every key, and the `tls.LoadX509KeyPair` slice the tool exercises (PEM decode, PKCS#8 and SEC 1 keys, the pair match check, the parent-cannot-sign refusal, and the issued validity clamped to the parent's NotAfter).  Documented divergences: dcrd's RSA4096 algorithm is omitted (the `rsa` crate's unfixed RUSTSEC-2023-0071 timing advisory, and no daemon component uses RSA), only the Decred-emitted key PEM forms are recognized, whole-second-UTC time rendering in the elapsed error vs Go's local-zone monotonic form, Rust OS-error texts vs Go's `*PathError`, and the hand-written help text without go-flags' default annotations.  Pinned by round-trip vectors (authorities across the algorithms re-loading through the CA loader, the issuance checks, the validity clamp, and a small-key RSA round trip) and CLI checks over the built binary culminating in a rustls/webpki-validated TLS handshake: a `-S -L` authority issues a localhost leaf that satisfies a real client trusting only the generated CA.  The Tor/proxy dial path runs in the daemon: a port of `decred/go-socks`'s `Proxy.DialContext` (the SOCKS5 greeting with optional RFC 1929 username/password authentication, the TCP CONNECT command always over a domain address so the proxy resolves the name, go-socks' exact reply-status error texts, and Tor isolation drawing fresh random hex credentials per connection) and of dcrd `connmgr.TorLookupIP` (Tor's SOCKS RESOLVE extension, command 0xF0, with dcrd's error-code table), assembled into the routing dcrd's `loadConfig` closures select over the pinned configuration enums — `--proxy` routes every dial through SOCKS and resolution through Tor unless `--noonion`, `--onionproxy` gives `.onion` traffic its own proxy, `dcrdDial` routes addresses containing `.onion:` to the onion dialer and `dcrdLookup` routes `.onion` hosts to the onion resolver, and `--noonion` makes both onion paths fail with dcrd's "tor has been disabled" — wired into the outbound connection driver's dials (a hostname address a proxied dial resolves remotely simply skips the address-manager attempt mark, which keys on parsed IPs) and `getaddednodeinfo`'s DNS lookup.  go-socks' `proxiedConn` bound-address dressing is read and discarded (the peer runtime keys everything on the dialed address), and the HTTPS seeder routes through the same dial path when a proxy is configured (dcrd routes its seeder transport through `dcrdDial`): a `ProxySeederTransport` dials each seeder through the `NodeDialer` — SOCKS or direct — wraps the connection in TLS validated against the public web PKI roots, and reads a minimal `Connection: close` HTTP/1.1 GET capped at the connmgr response limit, so a proxied operator's seeder traffic no longer leaks outside the proxy; without a proxy the battle-tested `ureq` transport still does the direct dial.  That minimal client follows no redirects and decodes no chunked transfer encoding (the Decred seeders answer 200 with the JSON body directly), a documented simplification from Go's `http.Client`.  The whole proxied request (dial, TLS handshake, request and response) runs under one absolute one-minute deadline that caps every socket read and write, as dcrd's per-seeder `context.WithTimeout(ctx, time.Minute)` does, so a peer trickling the handshake cannot hold a seeding round.  The direct `ureq` transport is built with `proxy(None)`, so neither transport takes a proxy from `ALL_PROXY`/`HTTPS_PROXY`/`HTTP_PROXY`, matching dcrd's seeder `http.Transport`, whose `Proxy` is nil.  Pinned by fake-proxy tests: the anonymous and authenticated handshakes bridging to a live echo target, Tor isolation's distinct per-connection credential pairs, go-socks' error texts, the Tor RESOLVE exchange with its error table, the `.onion` routing split between the onion proxy and direct dials, and the --noonion failures.  The pipe IPC lifecycle is wired end to end: the `--pipetx` writer thread serializes every queued message in the ported encoding (`serviceControlPipeTx`, with the no-descriptor drain), the `--piperx` watcher discards input until end-of-file or an error and then requests the same graceful shutdown as an interrupt (`serviceControlPipeRx` — the parent closing its end is the shutdown request), and under `--lifetimeevents` the daemon sends dcrd's events at dcrd's points.  Startup DbOpen goes out before the block database loads.  Startup P2pServer goes out ahead of everything dcrd does in `newServer` (the fee estimator, the chain load with its UTXO catch-up replay, the index catch-up, the address manager, the mempool, the listeners), so a server that fails to build has sent it and sends no P2pServer shutdown event.  Startup-complete goes out before the idle wait.  The P2pServer shutdown event follows the teardown and the final UTXO flush, after `Server shutdown complete` (dcrd's defer after `svr.Run`).  The DbOpen shutdown event goes out as the block database closes, on every exit after it opened, failures and the index-drop exits included.  dcrd's queue is an unbuffered channel whose sends wait for the writer; the port's queue never blocks a sender, so at exit the daemon instead waits up to a second for the writer to write what is queued (`PipeNotifier::wait_written`).  The workspace forbids `unsafe`, so the inherited descriptors cannot be adopted with `from_raw_fd`.  On Linux the daemon duplicates them from itself with `pidfd_getfd`, which shares the open file description as `os.NewFile` does, so the socketpairs Node/Electron parents pass work.  A descriptor left non-blocking is waited on with `poll`, for the `--piperx` reads and the `--pipetx` writes alike, as Go's poller does, and a failed `--pipetx` write logs `Failed to write to pipe` as dcrd does.  On kernels before 5.6, or under a sandbox that filters the call, the daemon falls back to re-opening through `/proc/self/fd`, which serves pipes and files but not sockets (`ENXIO`; `--piperx` then shuts down).  Other unixes re-open through `/dev/fd`, whose open duplicates the descriptor.  Adopting Windows pipe handles is an open gap (see Known remaining gaps).  Under `--boundaddrevents` the daemon sends one `p2plistenaddr` per bound peer-to-peer listener and then one `rpclistenaddr` per bound RPC listener (dcrd `initListeners` and `setupRPCListeners`, added in `fc017ce3`).  Each carries Go's `listener.Addr().String()`: IPv4-mapped addresses in dotted form, IPv6 bracketed, and a zoned link-local address with Rust's numeric scope where Go names the interface.  The RPC messages wait until the peer listeners have bound, which is the order dcrd's `newServer` binds the two in.  Before anything else, `main` raises the soft `RLIMIT_NOFILE` as the Go runtime does for dcrd before `main` runs (`syscall/rlimit.go`): to one below the hard limit.  On macOS Go clamps to `kern.maxfilesperproc`; with no safe sysctl, the port bisects for the largest value the kernel accepts, which is the same value for an ordinary user.  It then applies dcrd's `limits.SetLimits`: a soft limit above 2048 is kept, a lower one goes to 2048 (or to the hard limit if that is lower), and a hard limit below 1024 exits 1 with `failed to set limits: need at least 1024 file descriptors` before the configuration is read.  Until the review of 2026-09-23 the daemon raised nothing and ran at its inherited soft limit: 1024 under systemd, 256 in macOS shells.  A shutdown requested during startup is honoured at dcrd's `shutdownRequested` points.  The first is ahead of the block database load.  The second is after the load; dcrd has separate checks after `loadBlockDB` and after `LoadUtxoDB`, which are one here because the UTXO set lives in the block database.  The third is after the chain load and index catch-up, before any listener, dialer, seeder or background thread starts.  `Shutdown complete` is logged on every return, as dcrd's deferred log is.  The lifecycle log lines are dcrd's.  The `stop` RPC (only its first request is forwarded, as in dcrd), the `--piperx` close and the service stop log `Shutdown requested.  Shutting down...`, or `Shutdown requested.  Already shutting down...` for a repeat.  `Server shutting down` (SRVR, WRN) opens the teardown and `Server shutdown complete` (SRVR) closes it, followed by `Gracefully shutting down the block database...` and `Shutdown complete`.  `--allowoldforks` logs `Processing forks deep in history is enabled` (SRVR), then `--assumevalid` logs `Assume valid is disabled` or `Assume valid set to <hash>` (SRVR), in `newServer`'s order.  A bad `--assumevalid` value is `Unable to start server: invalid hex for --assumevalid: ...`.  The missing-config warning is logged under DCRD.  dcrd's `Gracefully shutting down the UTXO database...` has no counterpart, because the port keeps the UTXO set in the block database.  The fee estimator is built ahead of the chain, as in `newServer`, and a configuration it refuses fails startup as `Unable to start server: ...` with exit status 1.  After the peer listeners bind, the tail of dcrd's `initListeners` registers the node's own addresses with the address manager.  Every `--externalip` goes in at manual priority, its port defaulting to the network's; unparsable ports and unresolvable or unroutable hosts are skipped with dcrd's SRVR/AMGR warnings.  Without `--externalip`, every bound listener goes in at bound priority, and an unspecified bind expands to the same-family interface addresses: procfs on Linux, none elsewhere, the same enumeration gap the RPC certificate records.  Not ported from the lifecycle, each deliberate: the Go soft-memory-limit tuning (`GOMEMLIMIT`, garbage-collector headroom with no Rust analog), the pprof profile servers (`--profile`/`--cpuprofile`/`--memprofile` validate but serve nothing), and the pprof profile servers (`--profile`/`--cpuprofile`/`--memprofile` validate but serve nothing).  UPnP is not a gap: dcrd master removed port mapping and `--upnp` only logs that the option is no longer supported (`config.go:176`, `dcrd.go:74-78`), a warning the daemon reproduces verbatim.  And, and the misbehaving-mix-peer observer now runs: a ticker thread wakes at every mix epoch boundary (UTC time truncated to the epoch plus one epoch, dcrd `Observer.waitForEpoch`), skips the first boundary because no full epoch has finished, and hands each completed epoch to the pool's `check_prev_epoch` (dcrd `Server.Run` running `s.mixObserver.Run`), feeding the strike set behind `misbehaving_block`; the winning-tickets notification path consults that observer and refuses to prompt votes on a block spending misbehaving mix inputs (dcrd's `MisbehavingBlock` break in the same gated case).  The Windows service wrapper is ported over the `windows-service` crate in its own `dcroxide-winsvc` crate (the service-entry macro expands an FFI shim, so that crate denies rather than forbids unsafe while writing none itself): the dispatcher detects SCM invocation (dcrd `svc.IsWindowsService` folded into the failed-connect error) and runs the whole daemon under the service with dcrd's start-pending → running → stop-pending → stopped transitions, SCM stop/shutdown controls request the same graceful shutdown as an interrupt (dcrd `dcrdService.Execute`'s `shutdownRequestChannel` send, with a stop arriving before the daemon's shutdown channel exists latched and honored on arming), and the `-s/--service` commands install (manual start, dcrd's display name and description), remove, start (passing the process arguments through), and stop (waiting up to ten seconds for the stopped state) — with the option group registered only on Windows exactly like dcrd's `newConfigParser`, so the flag stays an unknown-option error on other platforms, pinned by the flags vectors.  As in dcrd's `loadConfig`, only the config-file pre-parse and the final parse carry the group, not the help pre-parse (dcrd's bare `flags.NewParser(&cfg, helpOpts)`, where `--service` is skipped as unknown).  The command runs straight after the version check, on that pre-parse alone: before the home directory or default config file is created, the config file read or anything validated.  It prints any error and exits 0.  Until the review of 2026-09-23 it ran only after a successful full load, so a bad config file blocked `--service=stop`, and on Windows the help pass took the option too, so `--bogus --service=stop` ran a command dcrd's pre-parse never reaches.  The `-h` help text is generated byte-for-byte as go-flags v1.6.1 renders it (the usage line, the Application Options section with the description column two past the longest long name and the value marker squeezing into the padding, `wrapText`'s last-space-within-width splitting with exact-width remainders kept whole and its hyphenated splits followed by a blank line, all over bytes as Go indexes them, so a split inside a multibyte character of a default path writes the raw partial bytes as dcrd does, the `(default: X)` note go-flags adds for every argument-taking option whose value in dcrd's defaulted config is not zero — rendered as `convertToString` does: the path or string, Go `Duration.String`, `FormatFloat` 'g' shortest, decimal integers, with the four `default-mask:"-"` passwords hidden — the `[$ENV]` annotations, and the Help Options tail with the trailing blank line of dcrd's `Println` over the help error), wrapped at the width go-flags' `getTerminalColumns` reads: the columns of the terminal on stdin (`TIOCGWINSZ` on descriptor 0, which the port reads through `stty size` over its own stdin because the workspace forbids unsafe code), or 80 when stdin is not a terminal or reports zero columns.  The descriptions are extracted verbatim from dcrd's config struct tags; dcrd's dedicated help pre-parse never adds the Windows service group, so neither does the render.  `tools/helpgen` regenerates the pinned vectors from the same struct filled with `loadConfig`'s defaults over a fixed `/home/user/.dcroxide` home: the 80-column one with stdin from /dev/null, and a 140-column one from a 140-column terminal.  On Windows go-flags wraps at the stdout console's `MaximumWindowSize.X` (`termsize_windows.go`), which needs an unsafe console call, so the port always wraps at 80 there and matches dcrd only when stdout is not a console.  The Windows event log half (`eventlog.InstallAsEventCreate`, the start-of-day message) is not ported.  A process the SCM starts has no standard handles, and std discards every write to them, so until the rotating log file is wired, a daemon run as a service keeps no log at all.  The SCM behavior compiles on Windows in CI but is exercised only by the command-dispatch and identity tests; running under a real service control manager is untested.  Documented daemon-layer divergences deferred from the cross-piece review of pieces 120-152: the accepted-block relay, fee-estimator enable, and background-generator gates evaluate netsync `IsCurrent` through a shared gate over the sync manager's is-current flag and sync height (dcrd's `isCurrent` is an `atomic.Bool` for the same cross-goroutine reads; the gate replicates `maybeUpdateIsCurrent` — current once latched, else the chain believing itself current with the best height at the sync height — without taking the manager lock, which those paths cannot); the peer-transport per-message read deadline is absolute (dcrd's `SetReadDeadline(now + IdleTimeout)` before each `readMessage`): the transport re-arms the socket timeout with the remaining budget before every receive, covering the header and payload of one message under a single wall-clock bound — during the handshake, whatever remains of the single negotiate deadline that bounds the whole exchange (dcrd's `Handshake` select on `time.After(negotiateTimeout)`, reported as `protocol handshake timeout` with the `ErrHandshakeTimeout` kind, pinned by `the_negotiate_timeout_bounds_the_whole_handshake`), and the idle timeout after — so a byte-dribbling peer cannot stretch a message read; the RPC request-read phase is bounded the same way plus a per-connection watchdog that closes the socket at the deadline (Go's `http.Server` `ReadTimeout` is an absolute bound at the conn level, under TLS too, where the rustls record loop cannot be bounded by re-armed receive timeouts alone), disarmed once the request is read; the RPC response write stays unbounded exactly as dcrd's `http.Server` without a `WriteTimeout` leaves it, and websocket connections live past the deadline by design (dcrd sets no read deadline on them), with the shutdown-time force-close bounding both; and the addrmgr upkeep now runs: a ticker thread dumps peers.json every ten minutes (the ticker half of dcrd addrmgr's `addressHandler`, with `savePeers`' dirty gate and the final save still at shutdown), and each outbound dial performs dcrd `attemptDcrdDial`'s full bookkeeping — the host/port split and net-address conversion over the configured lookup failing the dial like dcrd's, `AddAddresses` with the target as its own source so a `--connect`/addnode address the manager never learned joins it for gossip and persistence, then the `Attempt` mark.  The RPC listener drains on shutdown: per-connection handler threads register in a wait group entered on the accept thread before each spawn, and `RpcListener::shutdown` joins the accept threads (dcrd `Run`'s `wg` covers exactly its accept goroutines), lets the handlers finish through a one-second grace — websocket serving loops observe the shutdown flag within a poll interval, the port's analog of dcrd cancelling the request context — then force-closes any connection still alive (dcrd's per-client context watcher calling `wsClient.Disconnect`), so a mid-frame websocket stall or a zero-window reader cannot wedge the shutdown; the final wait is bounded, abandoning a still-stuck thread exactly as dcrd leaves in-flight standard handlers detached.  The RPC request parser decodes chunked `Transfer-Encoding` bodies the way Go 1.27's `internal/chunked` reader and `body.readTrailer` decode them: size lines end in CRLF, with no other CR; sizes are `parseHexUint`'s bare hex digits, at most 16; an extension is cut after trimming only spaces and tabs; the net non-data allowance is 16 KiB, in Go's wrapping `int64` arithmetic; a size line and the trailer section are each bounded at 4096 bytes; and trailer fields get the MIME-field checks, and at most 500 values.  It reads through a 4 KiB buffer rather than a byte per syscall pair, under the same authenticated read limit and deadline as `Content-Length` bodies.  A body the reader refuses is answered `400 error reading JSON message: <err>` with Go's error text, as dcrd's `jsonRPCRead` answers it, pinned by `chunked_framing_follows_gos_reader` and `chunked_overhead_is_bounded_where_go_bounds_it`, every row of which is a real server's answer.  It ignores the `Content-Length` of a chunked message per RFC 7230, and ignores `Transfer-Encoding` altogether below HTTP/1.1 as Go does (issue 12785, `transfer.go:646-649`); from HTTP/1.1 on it answers any other transfer encoding 501, exactly as Go's `net/http` does, with `chunked` matched by `ascii.EqualFold` over the untrimmed value.  Not reproduced: a body read cut short by the client's EOF or by the read deadline, outside the trailer, which dcrd answers `400 error reading JSON message: unexpected EOF` (or the timeout) and the port closes without an answer, in both framings (see Known remaining gaps).  The HTTPS seeder's shutdown no longer waits out an in-flight round: each seeder reports over a channel the retry loop polls against the stop signal, so a stop abandons the round immediately — dcrd cancels the requests through the daemon context, aborting them outright, while the port's abandoned request threads run to the one-minute transport timeout at most and die with the process, a documented divergence in the requests' fate but not in the shutdown latency.  Deferred from the P3 sweep of the same review: the UTXO flush is atomic: the entry writes land in the same database transaction as the utxo-set-state marker everywhere the cache flushes (dcrd's backend `PutUtxos` coupling) — the clean-shutdown flush additionally folds the modified block index rows and the best-state marker into that one transaction, the stats-time forced flush couples its marker the same way, and each disconnect's forced flush now records the parent tip as the set state exactly as dcrd's `MaybeFlush` call does, with the block's database spend-journal record removed in its own transaction only after that flush succeeds (dcrd's `dbRemoveSpendJournalEntry` intentionally after the forced `MaybeFlush`), so a crash between the best-state write and the flush still finds the journal record the cache needs to recover.  Manual peer control (`addnode`, `node connect`/`remove`) is wired end to end: the RPC connection-manager seams run dcrd's `rpcConnManager.Connect`/`RemoveByID`/`RemoveByAddr` over a control channel into the outbound dial driver (duplicate/resolve/max-peers checks in dcrd's order and error texts, the resolution on the RPC thread like `addrStringToNetAddr`, persistent-peer removal stopping the connmgr redial with the `CancelPending` fallback for a still-dialing added peer), with the `--connect` permanents resolved at startup exactly where dcrd's `newServer` resolves them (failing the start on an unresolvable address); dials run on per-request dialer threads reporting back to the manager (dcrd's `Connect` goroutines), so the dial scheduling — and the peer-control commands riding the same event loop — never block on an in-flight dial.  Target resolution follows the pin's `addrStringToNetAddr` and connmgr's `stdlibNetAddrToAddrMgrNetAddr`.  A Tor v3 host stays unresolved (dcrd's `simpleAddr`), so `--connect`/`--addpeer`/`addnode`/`node connect` accept it.  It is dialed over the onion route and served as an onion peer, and so are auto-dialed TorV3 addresses, which were previously torn down after connecting.  Every other host, IP literals included, resolves through the configured `dcrdLookup` routing, which under `--proxy` without `--noonion` is a Tor RESOLVE through the proxy.  The port parses with `strconv.ParseUint(port, 10, 16)`'s texts.  The seeders' source-address lookup (`querySeeders`' `dcrdLookup(seeder)`) takes the same routing, so a proxied daemon sends no clear DNS for peer or seeder names.  Where that routing ends at the system resolver (Go's `net.LookupIP`), a `.onion` name is never handed to it and comes back with no addresses: `no addresses found for <host>` for a connect target.  This is Go's `avoidDNS` (RFC 7686), which matches without regard to case and ignores one trailing dot.  Go's resolver additionally answers such a name from `/etc/hosts` and queries its `resolv.conf` search-list forms, so with a search domain configured dcrd answers `lookup <host> on <server>: no such host` instead.  The port sends no query at all.  The 2.2 campaign (upstream master `452c1a6c`) also lands the server smalls: the live ban flow — the misbehavior handlers now record bans in a shared banned-host map through dcrd `BanPeer`'s semantics (whitelist and disabled-banning short-circuits, host-keyed entries lifting after `--banduration`) at every dcrd ban site the dispatch serves: the threshold-tripped mempool/getdata/notfound scores (the notfound intake is newly dispatched, scoring per dcrd and forwarding survivors to the sync manager), dcrd 2.2's direct committed-filter ban (replacing the old +100 score, with master's reason text), and the direct empty-getdata/empty-inventory/empty-address-list/invalid-addrv2/malformed-mix bans — the direct sites keeping dcrd's only-disconnect-inside-`BanPeer` semantics so whitelisted peers and disabled banning stay connected; `addBanScore` follows dcrd 2.2 in accumulating whitelisted peers' scores (visible through getpeerinfo) while never banning them; and both inbound and outbound admission refuse banned hosts before any handshake work, clearing expired bans as they are consulted — pinned end-to-end by a dispatch test driving a ban and asserting the recorded host and the refused reconnect (peers whose frames fail wire decoding are banned too — the transport's read errors are typed so the read loop mirrors dcrd `OnRead`'s `wire.ErrorCode` ban, pinned by an end-to-end test sending a malformed frame and asserting the recorded host and refused reconnect); dcrd 2.2's retirement of the legacy mining-state exchange — getminings requests and miningstate messages from peers whose negotiated protocol version covers getinitstate ban with dcrd's exact reasons, as do repeated getminings/getinitstate requests (previously trace-ignored) and repeated initial state messages in either form, behind a new received-state latch, with the initstate intake now forwarded to the sync manager like dcrd `OnInitState`; and the ADXR logging subsystem removed to match master's `log.go` (the config vectors' resolved level maps and the supported-subsystems error updated in place).  The serve-signal plumbing carries owned disconnect reasons (`Cow<'static, str>`) so the dynamic 2.2 ban reasons flow without leaking |

## Deliberate divergences from dcrd: safety controls Go's runtime made unnecessary

dcrd's behaviour is the specification, and everywhere the two can agree they
do. The exceptions below are places where reproducing dcrd's *structure*
faithfully would reproduce a resource bound that only holds because of the Go
runtime. Each is a considered divergence, not an oversight, and each is
covered by a test that fails if the control is removed.

The recurring shape: a goroutine costs a few KiB and is scheduled by Go's own
runtime, so dcrd can spawn one per connection, queue into an unbounded
channel, and check its limits inside the handler. An OS thread costs a
multi-megabyte stack and a kernel scheduling slot, and a Rust channel that is
unbounded really is unbounded. Where dcrd's limit is enforced *after* the
expensive step, dcroxide moves it before.

| Area | dcrd | dcroxide | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Orphan eviction source | first entry of a `range` over a Go map — unpredictable per process, and dcrd's comment leans on that ("an adversary would have to be able to pull off preimage attacks … to target eviction of specific entries").  Unpredictable, but not uniform, and dcrd's comment is inaccurate about the mechanism: Go randomises the starting *slot*, not the starting *entry* (`internal/runtime/maps/table.go:710`, `:757-758`, `:1025`), so an entry is yielded first in proportion to the run of empty slots before it and the table is at most 7/8 full.  Measured over 300,000 ranges of a 100-entry map, the favoured entry led about 5.5x more often than uniform and the least favoured about 0.75x; eight entries is the one size that comes out uniform, because eight fill a group exactly | an index drawn from the injected `PoolChain::random_u64`, the OS CSPRNG in the daemon | The port stores orphans in a `BTreeMap`, which has no equivalent of Go's seeded map iteration: the first entry is always the numerically smallest transaction hash. Grinding a *large* hash costs milliseconds, so reading the first entry would let an attacker keep their own orphans resident indefinitely while honest low-hash orphans churn — inverting the property dcrd's comment claims. Drawing the index restores it. Any choice is conformant, dcrd's own being explicitly unspecified. Pinned by `eviction_follows_the_random_draw_not_the_hash_order` and `every_orphan_is_reachable_by_some_draw`. |
| Rival orphan trial order | the inner `map[chainhash.Hash]*wire.MsgTx` of `orphansByPrev` ranged in Go's per-execution randomized order (`mempool.go:1968-1990`), which is what makes tracking several children of one output the anti-griefing device dcrd describes it as | the collected rivals shuffled with Fisher-Yates from the injected `PoolChain::random_u64` before the acceptance loop | Only one child of a given output can be accepted — the loop takes the first that succeeds and breaks — so whatever orders the candidates decides which rival spend enters the pool. `orphans_by_prev`'s inner map is a `BTreeMap` keyed by child hash, so iterating it would hand the win to the numerically smallest hash every time, and an attacker reaches that by grinding a nonce for a handful of tries. This is the same hazard as the *Orphan eviction source* row above, at the selection site rather than the eviction site, and it takes the same remedy. Reported as RVW-019 by an external review of `382864f5`. Pinned by `trial_order_follows_the_draw_not_the_input_order`, `every_rival_can_be_tried_first` and `the_shuffle_preserves_every_rival`; those pin the shuffle itself, not `process_orphans_internal` end to end, which would need two rival transactions the fake chain accepts and the replayed dcrd corpus has no such scenario. |
| Rejected block bodies in memory | bodies are written to the database only (`process.go:331-337`), whose comment says keeping doomed-but-proof-of-work-valid blocks on disk is deliberate because proof-of-work makes filling the *disk* prohibitive; the sole in-memory copy is a fixed `recentBlockCacheSize = 12` LRU (`chain.go:43-48`) | the body is stored in the in-memory mirror by `maybe_accept_block_data` and dropped again by `forget_rejected_block_body` the moment a contextual or connect check rejects the block, when a database is attached | The port mirrors bodies in memory where dcrd does not, and `maybe_accept_block_data` stores before the contextual and connect checks run, so a rejected block was already resident. `prune_chain_memory` only reaches heights below `tip - 288`, and a peer feeding proof-of-work-valid blocks that fail a later check never moves the tip, so nothing it sent crossed the horizon and the map grew for as long as the flood ran — free on the reduced-difficulty networks, proof-of-work-gated on mainnet. Dropping at the point of rejection costs a database read on the rare re-request, which `block_data` already serves through `db_fetch_stored_block`; a chain with no database keeps the body, since there the map is the only copy. Reported as RVW-014 by an external review of `382864f5`. Pinned by `blocks_that_fail_validation_do_not_keep_their_bodies`, which replays dcrd's own battery: 102 of 104 validation-failed bodies stay resident without the drop, none with it. |
| Multisig builder size-limit error kind | `MultiSigScriptV0` returns `builder.Script()`'s error unchanged, which is txscript's `ErrScriptNotCanonical` -- a different package's error type crossing the boundary (`stdscript/scriptv0.go:848`) | re-labelled as stdscript's `ErrUnsupportedScriptVersion`, since the port's `Result<Vec<u8>, StdScriptError>` carries one error type and has no counterpart kind | Reached only with enough keys to exceed `MAX_SCRIPT_SIZE`, which is unspendable regardless because `OP_CHECKMULTISIG` caps the count far below it. Matching dcrd would mean the constructor's error carrying a txscript error across the package boundary, an API change for a script nobody can spend. The departure was already documented at the call site; it is recorded here so a ledger check finds it rather than re-deriving it, as RVW-022 of an external review of `382864f5` did. |
| Absurd amount conversion (`--minrelaytxfee`, RPC amounts) | `dcrutil.NewAmount` multiplies by 1e8 and converts to `Amount`. Go leaves an out-of-range float-to-int conversion implementation-defined; on amd64 `CVTTSD2SI` yields `MinInt64`. For the flag, dcrd ends up with a negative relay fee, and that stops it starting: `newServer`'s `MaxBucketFee` is `100 * MinInt64`, which wraps to 0, so `fees.NewEstimator` passes its first check and refuses with "minimum bucket fee rate cannot be <= 0". The same conversion takes client-supplied floats over RPC: `createrawtransaction`'s input `amount` (converted with no range check first, `rpcserver.go:819-826`) and `createrawssrtx`'s input `amount` and `fee` | `(f + 0.5) as i64` saturates, so the port ends up at `i64::MAX` (both copies of `new_amount`, `dcroxide-node/src/config.rs` and `dcroxide-rpc/src/handlers.rs`). Only a *positive* out-of-range amount differs; a negative one is `MinInt64` either way. For the flag, the estimator's maximum is dcrd's wrapping `100 *` product (`fees::new_shared_estimator`), which for `i64::MAX` wraps to -100, so `Estimator::new` refuses with "maximum bucket fee should not be lower than minimum bucket fee", logged as `Unable to start server: ...` with exit status 1. Over RPC, what clients see differs in three places. `createrawtransaction` serializes the input's `ValueIn` as `0x7fffffffffffffff` where dcrd/amd64 writes `0x8000000000000000`. `createrawssrtx`'s not-equal-to-ticket-amount error renders the saturated amount where dcrd/amd64 renders a negative one. Before the automatic-revocations agenda, its `fee` reaches `CreateRevocationFromTicket` as `MaxInt64`, not `MinInt64`. `createrawtransaction`'s output amounts fail the `MaxAmount` range check with the same text either way, and the `stdDev` fee helper cannot overflow | For the flag: local operator input only -- it takes a configured fee large enough to overflow the conversion, which no remote party supplies. Both nodes refuse to start; only the error text differs, because the conversion does. For the RPC sites: authenticated requests carrying amounts beyond the money supply, which get a garbage transaction or an error either way. In both cases there is no single dcrd behaviour to reproduce: Go's result is platform-defined, and on arm64, whose `FCVTZS` saturates, dcrd gets the port's `MaxInt64`, so a port matching amd64 would diverge from dcrd on another architecture. This row used to say a saturated fee rejects everything and a negative one accepts everything; neither node gets that far. Until the review of 2026-09-23 the port reached the refusal through an `expect` and aborted. Reported as RVW-011 by an external review of `382864f5` and closed deliberately rather than reconciled; the RPC sites were added to the row after the review of 2026-09-23. |
| `--authtype=clientcert` with an empty `--clientcafile` | no rule: `newTLSConfig` skips client verification when `clientCAsPath == ""` (`server.go:3689`), and the RPC server grants admin to clientcert sessions, so the endpoint serves unauthenticated admin RPC | `loadConfig` refuses the combination with `loadConfig: --authtype=clientcert requires --clientcafile`, but only when the RPC server will run; with `--norpc` the configuration loads as dcrd's does | Reproducing dcrd would hand every admin RPC to anyone who can reach the listener. The rule is scoped to where there is an endpoint to protect (`tests/review_config_notices.rs`, `clientcert_without_a_ca_file_is_refused_only_with_rpc_enabled`). |
| Non-UTF-8 config file values and environment variables | go-flags' ini reader keeps raw bytes (`ini.go` `readIni`), and `os.Getenv` returns a variable's raw bytes (WTF-8 on Windows), so a Latin-1 `rpcpass`, a non-UTF-8 `datadir`, or a non-UTF-8 `DCRD_APPDATA`, `HOME` or `$VAR` in a path is taken byte for byte | Lines dcrd skips (comments) may hold any bytes. An applied assignment whose line is not valid UTF-8 is refused with `Error parsing config file: <file>:<line>: the value of option <name> is not valid UTF-8`. An environment variable dcrd would read that is not UTF-8 is refused with `invalid UTF-8 in environment variable <NAME>: <value>` (followed by the usage line) before the load acts on it. Those variables are `HOME` for the default home, `DCROXIDE_APPDATA` and `DCROXIDE_ALT_DNSNAMES` when the option is not set, and a `$VAR` in `--datadir`/`--logdir` (`flags::getenv_utf8`, `config::load_config_from_argv_with_notices`). A variable dcrd leaves unread is never refused, and `-h`/`-V` still exit as dcrd's do. addblock refuses a non-UTF-8 `HOME` the same way once its configuration parses. This is the policy argv already follows (`flags::args_after_program`). | Config values are `String`s. The earlier lossy decode silently replaced each bad byte with U+FFFD, turning such a secret into a different one and such a path into a different directory. The environment lookups read such a value as unset, running the daemon on the default home, or for `HOME` on the current directory. Carrying raw bytes would need `OsString`/`Vec<u8>` fields throughout (`tests/review_config_notices.rs`). |
| `sendrawtransaction` with an explicit `null` `allowhighfees` | `ParseParams` fills `jsonrpcdefault` values only for absent trailing params, so an explicit `null` leaves `AllowHighFees` nil, and `handleSendRawTransaction`'s first statement, `allowHighFees := *c.AllowHighFees` (`rpcserver.go:4403`), is a nil-pointer panic. Over HTTP, net/http's per-connection recover drops the connection with no reply and the transaction is never decoded; over the websocket, `serviceRequest` runs in a bare goroutine with no `recover`, so the process dies. `sendrawtransaction` is on `rpcLimited`, so limited credentials suffice | reads the nil field as `false` and processes the transaction: the reply is its hash, and it is relayed and queued for rebroadcast. An omitted param gets dcrd's `false` default either way | Reproducing the panic is not an option under `panic = "abort"`: it would take the node down on both transports, which is worse than dcrd over HTTP. Reproducing only the HTTP connection drop would need a new close-without-reply outcome built through the HTTP and websocket layers for one upstream bug. The websocket crash is worth reporting upstream. Pinned by `sendrawtransaction_null_allowhighfees_reads_as_false` (`crates/dcroxide-rpc/tests/review_rpc_handler_parity.rs`). |
| `submitblock` bytes past the block | `dcrutil.NewBlockFromBytes` (`dcrutil/block.go:357-366`) decodes a block from the front of the submission and keeps the *whole* submission as the block's serialized bytes. ffldb's `StoreBlock` writes `block.Bytes()`, so any trailing bytes reach the flat files, and `getblock <hash> false` (hex of `blk.Bytes()`) echoes them back. P2P cannot produce this, because `ReadMessageN` rejects unconsumed payload bytes (`ErrTrailingBytes`), and peers are served a re-serialized `MsgBlock` | trailing bytes are dropped at decode: the block is stored in its canonical serialization, which `getblock <hash> false` returns | Persisting bytes chosen by the submitter serves no purpose; `submitblock` is on `rpcLimited`, so limited credentials suffice to do it. Reproducing it would mean carrying the raw submission through the sync manager and chain into the block store. Consensus never reads `Bytes()`, and `getblock`'s verbose `size` comes from the header, so only the raw hex and the flat-file contents differ. Pinned by `submitblock_trailing_bytes_are_not_kept` (`crates/dcroxide-rpc/tests/review_rpc_handler_parity.rs`). |
| Per-peer outbound queue | `outputQueue` (a 5000-slot `chan`) drained into `queueHandler`'s unbounded `pendingMsgs` slice; since `589dd4c5`, `queueOutMsg` charges each message its `msgSize` against the 40 MiB `maxQueuedOutputBytes` and disconnects a peer past it | `sync_channel(128)`, each message charged its framed size (dcrd's `msgSize`: `MessageHeaderSize` + `SerializeSize`, computed without encoding) against a 4 MiB `MAX_OUTBOUND_QUEUE_BYTES` budget released as the writer drains; a full queue — either ceiling — drops the message and logs once per congestion episode | An unbounded queue against a peer that stops reading is unbounded memory, and a count bound alone was coarse: 128 max-size blocks is ~46 MB per peer, so the byte charge makes the bound mean what it exists to bound (pinned by `outbound_queue_is_byte_capped_before_the_depth_cap`). The 4 MiB and 128-message ceilings are the port's own hardening, not dcrd's 40 MiB bound ported. Dropping rather than disconnecting is deliberate: an earlier revision that disconnected cut off honest peers on slow links. Producers must treat a refused send as not sent, so nothing records inventory the peer was never told about. A refused netsync getdata or getheaders goes back to the sync manager (`SyncManager::on_request_not_sent`). The manager re-requests each item from another peer known to hold it, or else forgets the request, rebuilding its needed-blocks list for a block, and it clears the getheaders duplicate filter. dcrd reaches the same outcome when its stall handler disconnects a peer that never answered; the port gets there without the wait. Pinned by `crates/dcroxide-netsync/tests/review_request_not_sent.rs` and `a_refused_sync_request_is_handed_back_for_the_manager`. The getdata serve worker never drops a reply the peer asked for. It waits for queue room, as dcrd's serve goroutine waits on its `maxPendingSend` semaphore, and stops only when the peer goes away. It charges each payload its real framed size (`a_refused_reply_waits_for_the_queue_to_drain`, `small_mix_replies_never_stall_the_send_pipeline`). While a reply waits, the worker logs one DEBUG line (`Outbound queue for peer P is full -- waiting to send CMD`), not the queue's `-- dropping` warning. **Reconciled against `589dd4c5`:** dcrd now bounds this too, tracking queued bytes per peer against a 40 MiB `maxQueuedOutputBytes` and *disconnecting* on exceed, so the divergence is no longer one of kind but of value (4 MiB vs 40 MiB) and action (drop vs disconnect). Both halves are kept deliberately. The value: 40 MiB across the default `maxpeers` of 125 is ~5 GB, the same order as the ~5.7 GB this row's byte charge exists to eliminate, so taking upstream's threshold would undo the bound rather than tighten it. The action: dropping still beats disconnecting for a merely slow peer, and the case dcrd's disconnect targets — a peer that has stopped reading altogether — is already handled here by the per-message write deadline in the *Peer socket writes* row below, which tears that connection down long before 40 MiB could pile up. What `589dd4c5` retires is only the old rationale that dcrd never disconnects on a full queue: it does now, at a threshold this port reaches by a different route. |
| Peer socket writes | 20 s plus one second per 256 KiB of the framed message, armed before every write (`peer/peer.go:84`, `:90`, `:1011-1019`, added by `62fd529a`); nothing at all before that commit | the same deadline, armed around every `write_message` (`WriteStallPolicy::dcrd()`, `transport.rs`) | Reproduced rather than divergent since `62fd529a`. Before it dcrd blocked in `Write` unbounded, which a goroutine can afford and an OS thread cannot; pinned by `the_write_deadline_matches_dcrds_formula`. A blocked goroutine costs a few KiB; a blocked OS thread costs a stack and never returns to the pool. |
| Per-peer threads and thread exhaustion | goroutines (`inHandler`, `queueHandler`, `outHandler` with its ping ticker, `stallHandler`, `serveGetData`, and the connmgr's dial goroutines and `time.Timer`s), which cannot fail to start | three OS threads per served peer (input, output, and one timer thread for the keepalive and the stall check, `run_peer_timers`) plus the getdata worker, started on first use. Every thread a remote peer's arrival causes (inbound serve, dial, outbound serve, the per-peer loops, the drop-log reset) starts through `runtime::spawn_conn_thread`. A refusal drops that connection before the server sees it (`DisconnectReason::ThreadRefused`) or fails that dial through the ordinary outcome path, and is logged at warn. The outbound driver's retry backoff and fill pause are deadlines its event loop waits on, not a sleeping thread each | `std::thread::spawn` panics when the OS refuses a thread (`EAGAIN` under `RLIMIT_NPROC` or a cgroup `pids.max`, `ENOMEM` for a stack), and the release profile aborts on panic. A unit whose task limit was sized for dcrd therefore turned one arriving peer into a crash loop. With every outbound slot held, the permit poll re-armed every 5 s, which meant a new OS thread every 5 s for the life of the node. Pinned by `a_refused_thread_drops_the_connection`, `a_refused_serving_thread_releases_the_admission`, `a_refused_dial_thread_reports_a_failed_dial` and `the_fill_wake_is_a_deadline_the_event_loop_keeps`. |
| Script validation worker start | `txValidator.Validate` starts `NumCPU()*3` goroutines, which cannot fail to start (`internal/blockchain/scriptval.go:120-132`) | one worker per core via `Builder::spawn_scoped`. The first thread the OS refuses stops the spawning, and the calling thread drains the remaining items through the same work-stealing index | `Scope::spawn` panics when a task limit (RLIMIT_NPROC, cgroup `pids.max`, `TasksMax`) refuses a thread, and release builds abort on panic, so reaching a limit mid-block took the node down. The verdicts are identical however many workers start; `validate::tests::validate_items_survives_refused_worker_threads` pins this. |
| RPC connection admission | goroutine per connection; `RPCMaxClients` checked inside the handler, after the TLS handshake | pre-authentication slot claimed on the accept thread against a two-tier budget — soft = `clamp(rpcmaxclients + 8, 32, 128)`, hard = `min(2 * soft, 256)`, both derived from a 512-thread process-wide ceiling at two threads per unauthenticated connection.
