At the soft tier one connection is disconnected to make room, chosen oldest first within three classes: first a connection already answered (the 401 or the 503) that is only discarding its declared body, since closing it loses nothing; then a connection that has not yet sent a request head; and as a last resort the oldest connection outright. Before the 2026-09-23 review answered connections counted as having sent a head and were protected, so a slow flood parked in the drain made a handshaking client the victim. The tier exists to shed arrivals that stall, and evicting a connection mid-service would leave it with no answer at all (pinned by `eviction_takes_an_answered_connection_before_a_handshaking_one`, `eviction_prefers_a_connection_that_has_not_sent_a_request` and `eviction_falls_back_to_the_oldest_when_all_have_sent_a_request`; until RVW-010 of an external review of `382864f5`, the selection ignored the head distinction); at the hard tier the arrival is refused with no reply | Connections that stall before sending a request never reach dcrd's 503, so under a flood each one costs a thread. The ceiling sits far above any admissible client count, so every state dcrd can reach — including its 503 — is reached identically. |
| RPC listen addresses | `setupRPCListeners` runs `parseListeners` (an empty host gives tcp4 and tcp6; a non-IP host gives "'%s' is not a valid IP address"). It binds each address with `net.Listen`/`tls.Listen`, which set `IPV6_V6ONLY` for tcp6 and `SO_REUSEADDR` outside Windows (`setDefaultListenerSockopts`). On a bind failure it warns 'Can't listen on %s: %v' and continues. `newServer` fails only with 'no usable rpc listen addresses'. | The same since the 2026-09-23 review (`start_rpc_listener` over `config::parse_listeners` and `runtime::bind_listener`). The daemon then logs 'RPC server listening on %s' once per listener, and reports a failure as dcrd's 'Unable to start server: %v'. The tcp6 bind sets `SO_REUSEADDR` outside Windows, as std's `TcpListener::bind` does for tcp4. Before a follow-up in the same review it did not: a restart within the `TIME_WAIT` period after IPv6 RPC traffic then lost the `::1` listener, and an IPv6-only rpclisten failed to start. The P2P tcp6 listener shares the helper and was affected the same way. | The warning's `%v` is Rust's `io::Error` text, not Go's `listen tcp4 ...: bind: ...`. The accept loop waits on listener readiness with a 50 ms cap, so a connection is accepted as soon as it arrives. Pinned by `review_rpc_listen` (including `a_restarted_v6_listener_rebinds_over_time_wait`) and by `review_rpc_startup::rpc_startup_logs_match_dcrd`. |
| Error-response body discard | discards after the response is flushed (`server.go:1701-1717`), gives up unread above `maxPostHandlerReadBytes` (`transfer.go:1002-1004`), and reads nothing at all when the request said `Connection: close` (`transfer.go:996-998`) | discards after the response and above the same cap, but discards a body already in flight even when the request said `Connection: close` | dcrd is reached through Go's own client stack; a raw client that sends its body and then blocks reading the reply loses it, because closing over an unread body resets the connection and the reset takes the queued response with it. Discarding a small, already-arrived body first is what makes the close clean, and costs only bytes no client can observe. The two arms that are reproduced exactly are the load-bearing ones: answering first, and reading nothing above the cap, without which a client that declares a `Content-Length` and never sends it is waited out until the handshake watchdog closes the socket and is answered with nothing at all. Pinned by `an_undelivered_body_does_not_swallow_the_401`. The mux's own answers (the 307, the `OPTIONS *` 200 and asterisk 400, the CONNECT 404), the `/ws` 401 and the 417 discard the same way, since Go runs `finishRequest` after them too (pinned by `mux_answers_discard_the_declared_body`). Two differences remain: Go's global options handler reads up to 4 KiB of an `OPTIONS *` body *before* its 200 (`server.go:4164-4175`), and so also sends a requested `100 Continue` there, where the port answers first and discards afterwards; and gorilla's handshake refusals (the 400/403/405 written by `serve_websocket`) still close without discarding. |
| Authenticated request body limit | `jsonRPCRead` reads through `io.LimitReader(r.Body, rpcReadLimitAuthenticated)` and never compares the declared length (`rpcserver.go:5678-5680`). It then calls `r.Body.Close()` before answering. Go's `body.Close` with `doEarlyClose` (`net/http/transfer.go:993-1019`) reads on through a chunked body for up to 256 KiB + 1 more decoded bytes. It reads on through a Content-Length body only when the request did not ask to close and at most 256 KiB remain. Otherwise it leaves the rest for the hijacked connection's close. `Close`'s error is dropped. | The same since the 2026-09-23 review. A Content-Length body is read up to `min(declared, 8 MiB)`. A chunked body stops at the limit, also when the limit falls on a chunk's end. `drain_body` or `discard_chunked_rest` then reads on under the read deadline as `body.Close` does, with `HttpHead::close` as Go's `Request.Close` (`shouldClose`). The two plain-text `400 request too large` answers are gone. | A valid request padded past the limit is served, and a truncated one gets -32700 inside a 200, as dcrd answers. Where Go reads the remainder, the close is now a FIN rather than a reset, and the answer waits on the remainder up to the read deadline, as dcrd's does. The handshake watchdog stands down before that discard, because at the deadline it shuts the socket both ways and would lose the answer. The per-read deadline still bounds the discard, except for a TLS record an authenticated client dribbles in. One timing-dependent case remains: Go's chunked reader parses the CRLF and the next size line in the same `Read` when they are already in its buffer, so a malformed line arriving with the last byte before the limit can still get dcrd's 400. The port always stops at the limit. Pinned by these tests in `review_rpc_listen`: `an_oversized_content_length_is_truncated_and_served`, `an_oversized_chunked_body_is_truncated_and_served`, `a_keep_alive_body_just_past_the_limit_is_read_to_its_end`, `a_chunk_ending_at_the_limit_is_served_whatever_follows` and `a_stalled_body_past_the_limit_is_answered_at_the_read_deadline`. Also pinned by `rpcrun::tests::request_close_is_gos_should_close`. |
| Websocket handshake carrying a body | gorilla inspects the hijacked reader and closes the connection outright on any byte that arrived alongside the handshake -- "client sent data before handshake is complete" (`gorilla/websocket@v1.5.1 server.go:186-191`) -- which dcrd surfaces only as a log line, never as a response (`rpcserver.go:6009-6015`) | refuses when the request *declared* a body in either framing, closing the same way and with the same silence | `read_http_head` takes the head one byte at a time and stops on the blank line, so nothing is ever buffered past it and there is no arrival to inspect -- the declared framing is the testable equivalent. It is stricter for a body declared but not yet sent (gorilla upgrades) and looser for bytes pipelined with no `Content-Length` (gorilla refuses), and identical for the only shape that can desync a front end: a `Content-Length` a proxy forwards as a request body while this server reads those bytes as the first RFC 6455 frames. Without the check the upgrade is answered 101 and the body is consumed as frame data. Pinned by `a_websocket_upgrade_declaring_a_body_is_refused`. |
| Request head size cap | `MaxHeaderBytes` is never set (`rpcserver.go:5917-5934`), so Go's default applies: heads up to `DefaultMaxHeaderBytes + 4096` -- a megabyte and change -- are served, and only past that is it `errTooLarge` -> 431 (`server.go:939`, `:960-962`, `:2067-2076`) | 431 with Go's exact wording, but at `MAX_HEAD_SIZE` = 8 KiB | the head is read byte at a time into a buffer held for the whole handshake, and a pre-authentication slot is held with it, so the cap is a live per-connection memory bound multiplied by the admission ceiling rather than a Go-runtime detail. A megabyte apiece across the hard budget is 64 MiB an unauthenticated client can pin. The status, the body, and the position of the check in the order are Go's; only the threshold is not, so a head between 8 KiB and a megabyte -- a large `Authorization`, a long cookie jar, a proxy stacking `Via`/`Forwarded` chains -- is refused here and served by dcrd. Pinned by `connection_level_errors_are_go_shaped`. Go 1.27 also caps the header *count* at 500 (`server.go:941-958`); there is no equivalent here. |
| RPC mux surface | Go 1.22's routing tree behind `ServeMux`: wildcards, `{$}`, method and host patterns, the trailing-slash redirect, and 405 with `Allow` (`net/http/server.go`, `routing_tree.go`, `pattern.go`) | the same *observable* pipeline reduced to the two patterns dcrd registers (`rpcserver.go:5936`, `:5961`): the asterisk arm, `EscapedPath`, `cleanPath` over the escaped string, the canonicalizing 307, and a per-segment decoded comparison against `/ws` | the tree's other arms are unreachable with two literal, method-less, host-less patterns. The reduction is checked rather than assumed: `tests/data/muxroute.tsv` holds 3,836 targets and the answer a real `http.Server` carrying dcrd's exact registrations gave each one, and `routing_matches_go_over_the_generated_corpus` replays every row. **A third registered pattern invalidates the reduction.** Not reproduced, and unreachable from any HTTP client: a raw non-UTF-8 byte in the target, which the head parser refuses because the head is held as `&str`. The request target goes through a port of Go 1.27's `url.ParseRequestURI`: the control-byte refusal, `getScheme`, the opaque form, and `parseAuthority`/`parseHost` with netip's IPv6 grammar and the http/https strict-colon rule. CONNECT's `http://`-prefixed authority form is ported too, and `Request.Host` is taken from an absolute-form target's host. `request_targets_parse_as_gos_request_uri` replays a second, probe-generated table of 155 targets together with the `URL.Host` each names. The head is read as `readRequestLimit` and textproto read it: lines end at a bare LF, as `bufio.ReadLine` ends them; a first field line that begins with a space or tab is refused, with a bare 400, or the 431 once it runs past the 80 bytes Go reads to quote it; a later line that begins with a space or tab continues the field before it (obs-fold), each line trimmed and the lines joined with one space; the colon must be on a field's first line, and the name must be non-empty; at most 500 values are read before the 431; and the checks Go makes once the header map is read follow in Go's order — a second Host, then the transfer encoding, the content length and `fixTrailer`'s trailer keys, then the version, then a missing Host (excused for the bare `PRI * HTTP/2.0` preface, per `isH2Upgrade`), a malformed Host and a space in a field name. Pinned by `field_lines_fold_as_textproto_folds_them`, `the_head_carries_at_most_gos_value_count` and `head_errors_rank_as_gos_do`; every row is a real server's answer. The corpus, those tables and the port all reflect Go 1.27. A dcrd built with Go 1.26, the other half of its CI matrix, answers differently in three places, the last two checked against Go 1.26.5's source. The redirect: 1.26 builds `url.URL{Path: ...}` and so re-escapes `%`, so `//%2f/ws` redirects to `/%252f/ws`, where 1.27 and the port send `/%2f/ws`. The value cap: 1.26 caps neither the head's nor the trailer's header values (`DefaultMaxHeaderValueCount` is new in 1.27: `api/go1.27.txt`, go.dev/issue/79936), and reads any number within its 1 MB header limit. The long leading-space line: 1.26 answers a leading-space first field line over 80 bytes with a bare 400, since mapping textproto's "message too large" to the 431 (`request.go:1152-1157`) is also new in 1.27. |
| Connection reuse, and the `Connection` header that advertises it | keeps the connection alive after the five answers that carry no `Connection` header -- the mux's 307, its CONNECT-authority 404, the `OPTIONS *` 200, and the `/ws` 401 and gorilla handshake refusals -- and closes after every `/` answer, where the handler sets `Connection: close` itself (`rpcserver.go:5937`). The read deadline is per request and resets, so a reused connection lives indefinitely | one request per connection everywhere, so every reply names `Connection: close` and the socket is then closed | measured, not assumed. Reuse was designed and then declined: it converts a pre-authentication surface that costs an attacker a TCP (and TLS) handshake per reply into a bandwidth-limited reflector -- **6.2x amplification measured**, 30-byte requests to 186-byte replies, unlimited pipeline depth, no authentication -- and lets one unauthenticated client hold a connection open forever at roughly 4 B/s by resetting the per-request deadline (48 s observed, resettable without limit). dcrd absorbs both because a goroutine is cheap and `/ws` traffic is not counted by `numClients` at all (`rpcserver.go:5445-5461`); this port has a pre-auth slot budget precisely because it cannot make that trade, which is the same reasoning as the admission row above. Reuse would also raise slot-seconds for a `/ws` handshake refusal by about four orders of magnitude (a released slot at ~1 ms becomes one held for the full 10 s), leave the shutdown drain about 50 ms of margin against `HANDLER_DRAIN_GRACE`, and require `Connection: close` detection to reproduce the *union* of two different Go scanners (`httpguts.HeaderValuesContainsToken` over every copy for `r.Close`, and `net/http`'s `hasToken` over the first copy only). The header is downstream of the behaviour: omitting it while still closing would advertise a reuse that never happens and leave a client to meet the close as an unexpected EOF. Reachability is the other half of the trade -- browsers do not follow redirects on a `ws://` handshake and gorilla does not retry a 401 on the same connection, so no real client reaches the five reusable answers. `Date` *is* reproduced, on exactly the answers Go writes through its `ResponseWriter`. It is on none of the ones written straight to the socket: the connection loop's own answers, and the JSON-RPC reply dcrd writes by hand on the hijacked connection (`rpcserver.go:5858`). That reply also carries no `Content-Length` and runs to the close. Pinned by `the_date_header_follows_who_wrote_the_response`. |
| Websocket handshake refusals to an HTTP/1.0 or HEAD request | gorilla's refusals go through `http.Error` (`returnError`). The status line mirrors an HTTP/1.0 request's protocol (`server.go:1562`, `:1586-1600`), and a HEAD gets the `Content-Length` but no body (`server.go:377-384`, `:1382`) | `write_handshake_error` in `websocket.rs` answers `HTTP/1.1` and sends the body whatever the request | Every answer `rpcrun.rs` writes follows the request's method and protocol: the JSON-RPC 200, the 401, 503, 404 and 307, the asterisk answers, the chunked-body 400 and the 417 (pinned by `answers_follow_the_request_method_and_protocol` and `head_and_http_1_0_answers_are_shaped_as_gos`). The websocket module's own writer was outside that fix. A websocket client sends neither HTTP/1.0 nor HEAD, so no real client reaches the difference. |
| Wire payload allocation | `readMessageN` checks the global cap, magic, command, and the per-type maximum before `make([]byte, hdr.length)` | same order, via `wire::read_message_header` called before the buffer resize | Not a divergence but a *correction*: the port had been bounding the allocation by the 32 MiB global cap alone, letting a peer name 32 MiB in 24 bytes. `read_message` now delegates to the same function so the order stays single-sourced. |
| Websocket client registration | `defer` removes the client | RAII `ClientRegistration`; whole handler inside one `catch_unwind`, recovery replies with a null id | Go's `defer` runs on panic; a trailing statement does not. A panic on the request id previously re-panicked in the recovery arm and leaked the client slot permanently. |
| Unwired RPC seams | every method is implemented | The seam traits' defaults answer a JSON-RPC internal error, or a neutral value where the signature cannot fail; they exist for the handler test doubles. The daemon's `NodeRpcChain` overrides every `RpcChain` method a handler reaches, and `NodeRpcConnManager` overrides `broadcast_message`. Until the 2026-09-23 review, fourteen chain seams and the broadcast were left at their defaults: getstakedifficulty, getblocksubsidy at or below the tip, estimatestakediff, ticketvwap, getstakeversions, getstakeversioninfo, getvoteinfo, gettreasurybalance, ticketsforaddress, invalidateblock and reconsiderblock always failed; a mined tspend read as unknown to gettreasuryspendvotes; getchaintips answered `[]`; and `ping` and ws `rebroadcastwinners` sent nothing. The three mixing seams (`mix_pooler`, `accept_mix_message`, `relay_mix_messages`) were at their defaults too and now run over the shared mixpool (see the RPC handlers row). getchaintips now sorts as dcrd's `sort.Reverse(nodeHeightSorter)` does: descending height, with ties broken by descending raw hash bytes. | A remote caller must never be able to unwind a handler thread, so a default is an error value. Pinned against the real adapter by `crates/dcroxide-node/tests/review_rpc_seams.rs` (including `equal_height_chain_tips_sort_by_descending_hash`) and `rpcrun::tests::broadcast_message_queues_to_every_peer_and_stamps_pings`. |
| Autogenerated secret files | `0600` file in a `0700` directory (`config.go` 471/506, `server.go` 3592) | same, applied at `open(2)`/`mkdir(2)` time rather than by a follow-up `chmod`, and a key that cannot be placed is fatal | Also a correction rather than a divergence: `fs::write` has no mode argument, so the port had been creating both the credential file and the TLS private key world-readable. |
| Per-peer stall detector | `stallHandler` goroutine fed by a `stallControl` channel | shared `Mutex<StallDetector>` ticked by the per-connection timer thread it shares with the keepalive ping (`run_peer_timers`) | The state is the point, so it lives behind its own mutex; the I/O loops never block on a control channel and dcrd's `select { stallControl <- msg; <-quit }` race disappears. Which peers get disconnected, and when, is dcrd's. |
| getdata ban score | `numNewReqs*99/wire.MaxInvPerMsg` in Go integer division, so batches of 505 items or fewer are free | identical, truncation included | An earlier revision carried the remainder so repeated 505-item batches were not free. Reverted: at 0.00198 points/item against dcrd's 60s half-life and threshold of 100, the equilibrium is ~583 items/s — and both daemons request blocks in batches of `maxInFlightBlocks` (16), which truncates to zero, so dcrd charges an honestly syncing peer nothing while a carry charges the full rate. At ~1 KiB early-chain blocks that is ~0.6 MB/s of upload, i.e. ordinary IBD. The getdata path is bounded by the pending-batch/pending-item gates and the send pipeline instead. |
| Repeated items within one `getdata` | `handleServeGetData` walks `invVects` verbatim (`server.go:532`), so a hash listed N times is fetched N times, queued N times, and appears in the consolidated `notfound` N times; only the continuation inventory is emitted once, because `continueHash` is cleared after the first match. The dcrd-generated `dupcontinue` row in `crates/dcroxide-node/tests/data/srvservedata_vectors.txt` records exactly that: `block, inv, block`. | the serve worker (`GetDataWorker::serve_batch`) resolves and serves each `(type, hash)` at most once per batch. A repeat is still charged the same single `numPendingGetDataItemReqs` decrement it would have been charged had it been served, so the counter the intake gate reads stays bit-for-bit dcrd's; unknown inventory types are excluded from the dedupe, so dcrd's decrement-free `continue` for them is untouched. The pure `serve_get_data` fold in `server.rs` deliberately does **not** dedupe — it is the model checked against the dcrd oracle. | A 50,000-item `getdata` costs 1.8 MB on the wire; naming one hash 50,000 times makes dcrd return up to 50,000 x `MaxBlockPayload` (1.25 MB), a ~36,000x amplification, and dcrd's 12-block `recentBlocks` LRU (`internal/blockchain/chain.go:48`) is never populated by the serve path, so any older hash is a full database read and deserialize per copy. Pinned by `getdata_serves_repeated_items_once`. |
| Peer stall deadlines | `stallHandler` goroutine fed by a `stallControl` channel, `pendingDeadlines` holding `pendingData` per `wire.InvVect` plus `pendingCmds` per command | same two tables, behind a shared `Mutex<StallDetector>` ticked by the per-connection timer thread it shares with the keepalive ping (`run_peer_timers`) | Ported from master, replacing the `release-v2.1.5` command-keyed table where any single delivery settled every request a `getdata` was waiting on — a peer could answer one block just inside the timeout and keep all 16 sync slots pinned. Master's `maxBurst` refusal is ported too. Two shape differences: the mixing-message identity hash is computed by the reader and passed in rather than cached on the wire type (dcrd caches it in `readMessage`; dcroxide keeps mix hashing in `dcroxide-mixing`), and the burst refusal is logged where it happens because ending the output loop shuts the socket down, so the connection's own teardown reason reports the resulting end of stream instead. |
| Address-manager bucket key, connmgr CSPRNG seed | `crypto/rand` | `getrandom` | The port had been deriving both from the clock, making bucket assignment predictable from a node's approximate start time. Anyone who ran an affected build should delete `peers.json`. |
| Mempool orphan-eviction draw | nothing: `limitNumOrphans` takes the first entry of a `range` over a Go map (`internal/mempool/mempool.go:490-501`), and dcrd's mempool imports no randomness package at all | a `SystemCsprng` stream seeded at `NodePoolChain` construction | The port stores orphans in ordered containers, so the unpredictability dcrd gets free from map iteration has to be asked for. What is borrowed is the *source's* failure contract, not the seam: fatal at initial seeding, infallible afterwards. Reading the kernel per draw instead — as this did — put a fallible call on a path a peer paces by pushing orphans past `max_orphan_txs`, and under `panic = "abort"` an entropy failure there is an outage dcrd's map walk has no way to cause. |
| `Prng` stream lifetime | rekeys every 4 MiB or 20 s (`crypto/rand/prng.go:20-21`, `:84-104`) | rekeys every 4 MiB only | The byte budget is not hygiene here, it is a bound on a reachable panic: the port's `chacha20::ChaCha20` is the 96-bit-nonce variant with a `u32` block counter, and `apply_keystream` panics rather than erroring at `(2^32-1)*64` bytes. The 20-second reseed is not reproduced — it is unobservable, and implementing it would put a clock read on every draw where the byte budget alone already removes the panic. Note also that `PRNG.seed` draws its 32-byte mix off the outgoing cipher without charging it (`prng.go:72`, counter cleared at `:78`), so one cipher yields `maxCipherRead + 32` bytes; reproduced rather than corrected, and nothing upstream documents a per-cipher budget for that to contradict. |
| Entropy failure after startup | impossible to observe: `crypto/rand.Read` calls `runtime.fatal` and kills the process rather than returning an error on any toolchain dcrd can build with (`go.mod` requires 1.25), so `PRNG.seed`'s `if err != nil && p.t.IsZero()` guard (`prng.go:68-70`) is dead code | `Prng::reseed` ignores a failed `getrandom` and rekeys from the outgoing keystream | Deliberate, and the one place this port is more available than upstream. dcrd's *documented* contract is the infallible draw ("Read never errors", `prng.go:84`; "will never panic after package init", `README.md:18`), which is what the 4 MiB rekey exists to provide; aborting instead would trade an unreachable divergence for a reachable process kill. Unpredictability is unaffected — the replacement key is a function of cipher state an observer without the old key cannot compute. |
| Reduction of a draw to a range | `ShuffleSlice`/`IntN` reduce through the bias-free `Uint64N` (`crypto/rand/uniform.go:102-148`): Lemire multiply-shift against a `-n % n` rejection threshold | `dcroxide_crypto::rand::Prng::uint64n` is that same reduction, line for line, and the peer environment's address shuffles go through it via `rand::shuffle_slice`. `dcroxide_connmgr::SystemCsprng::uint64n` and `dcroxide_addrmgr::SystemRng::int_n` reach the same distribution by rejection sampling instead, and `dcroxide-mempool/src/pool.rs:748` (`limit_num_orphans`) still reduces a full-width draw by plain modulo | Enumerated rather than counted, because three successive attempts to state this as a closed set were each wrong. Raw modulo over a random draw, in production code, is exactly two, both in the mempool: `dcroxide-mempool/src/pool.rs:748` (orphan eviction in `limit_num_orphans`) and `:394` (`shuffle_trial_order`). **Neither is a divergence, and neither is going to be converted.** dcrd draws nothing at either site — it ranges over a Go map — so there is no upstream reduction to converge on, and the *Orphan eviction source* row above records what that map walk actually distributes: off uniform by a factor of several, where this modulo is off by about one part in `2^57`. Adopting `Uint64N` here would buy distance from dcrd's real distribution while appearing to close a gap. It would also put a rejecting, redrawing loop behind `PoolChain::random_u64`, which this crate's test doubles answer with a constant: `2^64 mod n` is nonzero for every non-power-of-two bound, including the default `max_orphan_txs` of 100, so such a source would never clear the threshold and the pool would hang rather than fail. The modulo's totality is a property of this seam, not an oversight, and the trait's doc comment now says so. Converted where dcrd's own arithmetic was the target: the peer address shuffles, the mining-address pick, the rebroadcast timer's `rand.Duration` (`rebroadcast.rs` `rand_duration`, now `dcroxide_crypto::rand::duration` over `Prng::uint64n`, where it used to reject-and-retry over kernel draws), and the seeder's backdating, which was the largest of them — four days in nanoseconds (`~2^48`) is a bias near one part in `2^16`, roughly `2^41` times the mempool's. Bias-free but not Lemire, and so still divergent in arithmetic and draw count though not in distribution: `dcroxide_connmgr::SystemCsprng::uint64n` and `dcroxide_addrmgr::SystemRng::int_n`, which both reject-and-retry. What remains is those two rejection loops, which are bias-free but map stream bytes to outputs differently from Lemire and consume a different number of draws (unobservable: the input is an unpredictable keystream, so nothing outside the process can tell which arithmetic produced a value), and the two mempool modulo reductions above, whose bias is `2^64 mod n` in `2^64`: about one part in `2^57` for the default 100 orphans, a limit that also bounds the rival lists `shuffle_trial_order` permutes. |
| Metadata store after a failed durable write | no latch: `dbCache.flush` returns the commit error without clearing the cached treaps (`database/ffldb/dbcache.go:501-509`), so the next flush retries the same dirty set and can return success | the failure latches the store fatal and every later write on that handle is refused with `ErrorKind::Fatal` (`ErrFatal`, the one error kind with no dcrd counterpart); reads keep answering from whatever redb can still read. After an I/O error redb fails every uncached page with `PreviousIo` until restart; the UTXO reads report that as an error, and the ffldb-layout reads answer absence, as ffldb does. The latch is set before a failing commit releases the writer semaphore, and it is checked again once `begin`, `Database::flush` or `Database::close` holds the semaphore, so a writer queued behind the failure cannot re-run the failed flush | A retry that succeeds after a failed commit is how a store loses data silently: the engine reports durability the log will not honour, and `Chain::flush` writes block index rows, UTXO entries and both state markers in one transaction precisely so a crash cannot leave them disagreeing. Not a runtime artifact either — the trade is availability for integrity, which is the right way round for a daemon that already runs under a supervisor because release builds abort on panic. Reads are deliberately not latched: bytes that reached disk are still valid, and refusing to serve them turns a write fault into a total outage for no gain. `Database::is_fatal` is threaded into the block-processing result (`sync.rs:166`) so a storage fault is not reported as a consensus rule error against the peer that sent a perfectly good block, and a failed shutdown flush exits `FAILURE` rather than success. Pinned by the latch's own tests, the source-scanning durability test, and the ENOSPC fault injection. |
| Shutdown UTXO flush failure | `ShutdownUtxoCache` discards `MaybeFlush`'s error (`internal/blockchain/utxocache.go:1062`), so `dcrdMain` exits 0 | logs `Unable to flush the block database: ...` and exits 1; `Server shutdown complete`, both shutdown events and the database close still follow | By then the store has latched fatal (row above), and exit status 0 would tell a supervisor the node's state reached disk. Nothing on disk is inconsistent: the store holds the last completed flush, and the next start replays forward from it (`initialize_utxo_state`). |
| Commit that trips a cache flush | `commitTx` flushes and then writes the transaction's own rows straight to the persistent database with `commitTreaps` (`database/ffldb/dbcache.go:558-582`), so the triggering write is durable when the commit returns | the flush publishes first and the transaction's rows land in the overlay after it, so the commit that trips a flush returns before its own rows are durable | Nothing in the chain treats a returning commit as durable: the barrier is the explicit `db.flush()` at the end of the UTXO cache flush, and recovery already tolerates losing that whole window — a strictly larger loss. Writing through instead would let a reader that seeds between the flush's commit and its retirement observe the triggering transaction partially applied, and hold that snapshot for its lifetime; the RPC block-region path seeds outside the chain lock, which is what the split flush exists to allow. Real parity here is a reorder, not a write-through: publish the rows before starting the flush so they ride inside the captured layers. That is gated on the fatal-latch fix, because a failed flush would otherwise leave the rows cached while their block bytes rolled back. |
| Block index flush on failure | `blockIndex.Flush` clears the modified set only after a successful `db.Update` (`blockindex.go:1416-1432`), so a failed flush is retried by the next one | `take_block_index_rows` drains the set through `BlockIndex::take_modified` *before* the write | A failed flush loses those rows permanently here, a strictly worse worst case than the revalidation dcrd's own NOTE describes (`chain.go:1509-1511`).  Affects all five callers, not only the warn-only path.  Not changed by the warn-only logging work; recorded so the new warning is read as the symptom it is. |
| Warn-only flush placement | `InvalidateBlock` goes from `MarkBlockFailedValidation` (`process.go:721`) straight to the isCurrent latch reset, next flushing at `:767` | `invalidate_block` performs an extra warn-only flush at `process.rs:3510` with no dcrd counterpart, and both `invalidate_block` and `reconsider_block` flush before their reorganization where dcrd flushes after | The warning count matches — one line, same text and level, from a different site — but not because of the empty-set short-circuit, which cannot fire at `:3510`: `mark_block_failed_validation` marks the node through `set_status_flags`/`unset_status_flags` on the line above, so that flush always has a row to write.  What equalises the count is the drain-before-write divergence in the row above: `:3510` drains the set, so the counterpart flush at `:3555` finds it empty and stays silent where dcrd's `:767` warns.  Both flushes are on `invalidateblock`, which the daemon serves since the 2026-09-23 review, so an operator reaches them.  The warning count still matches dcrd's. |
| `invalidateblock` / `reconsiderblock` RPCs | live handlers; the chain's notifications drive mempool, index and websocket maintenance whoever reorganized | Live handlers since the 2026-09-23 review. `NodeRpcChain` calls `Chain::invalidate_block`/`reconsider_block`, then drains the daemon's `ChainNtfnHandler` with the chain mutex released, as the sync adapter does after a processed block. | An operator's `invalidateblock`/`reconsiderblock` therefore reaches the warn-only flush placement in the row above, not only `force_head_reorganization`. Pinned by `review_rpc_seams::invalidate_and_reconsider_move_the_tip`. |
| Tor SOCKS RESOLVE: two implementations, one live | one `TorLookupIP` (`addrmgr/tordns.go:44-137`), reached through `cfg.lookup`/`cfg.onionlookup` with `context.Background()` and no socket deadline, reading each reply head with a single unchecked `conn.Read`, and accepting any IPv6 answer longer than the six-byte minimum — `if replyLen <= 4+2 { ... }` then `addr = net.IP(reply[0:replyLen-2])`, so a nine-byte reply yields a seven-byte `net.IP` | two. `dcroxide-addrmgr`'s `tor_lookup_ip` reproduces all of the above exactly over a `TorTransport` seam, returning `Vec<Vec<u8>>` so an off-length answer is representable; it is reached only by its frozen vectors. The daemon calls `dcroxide-node`'s own (`src/socks.rs`), which reads the reply heads with `read_full`, carries one absolute deadline across the whole exchange, and rejects an IPv6 answer whose address half is not exactly sixteen bytes — with dcrd's own "invalid IPV6 address" text | The daemon's lookup returns `std::net::IpAddr`, which cannot hold a seven-byte address, so the length check is forced by the type rather than chosen; dcrd's malformed `net.IP` fails to render or connect downstream anyway, so the observable difference is an error here against a useless address there. The deadline and the checked reads run in the port's favour and are deliberate: dcrd sets no deadline, so its Tor lookup can pin a caller indefinitely. Both halves pinned — the daemon's by `the_daemon_resolver_refuses_an_ipv6_answer_dcrd_would_accept`, dcrd's by `seed_tor_vectors`. |
| Mixpool key exchanges per identity | none: a key exchange needs only its sender's own pair request known and may reference arbitrary other hashes (`acceptKE`, `mixing/mixpool/mixpool.go:1668`), so one accepted pair request can mint unlimited sessions, each held until the pair request goes | `MAX_KES_PER_IDENTITY` = 32 key exchanges held per identity; the 33rd is refused with a non-bannable rule error, neither pooled nor relayed | Otherwise one UTXO buys remote memory growth at line rate. Honest headroom: a wallet forms one run per epoch tick for its pair request's life (about 6 mainnet and 20 testnet3 epochs) plus in-epoch reruns. Pinned by `key_exchange_flood_stops_at_the_per_identity_cap` (`crates/dcroxide-mixing/tests/mixpool_expiry.rs`). |
| Un-orphaned mix messages | `reconsiderOrphans` accepts every orphan by the identity carrying the new key exchange's session ID (`mixpool.go:1585-1629`) without the direct path's same-type conflict check (`:1294-1307`), so up to an orphan pool's worth (`maxOrphans` = 250) of one message type is pooled and relayed for one identity and session | the conflict check runs there too: the earliest-received orphan of each type is accepted, and later ones of a type the identity already holds in the session are dropped from the orphan pool; orphan key exchanges stay orphans there, as in dcrd | Without it the per-identity key exchange cap bounds nothing else: 32 sessions × ~250 slot reservations of up to ~0.5 MB each per identity, held for about an hour. Honest senders send one message of each type per session and never reach it. Residual, as in dcrd: one UTXO can back several identities (a pair request that claims an outpoint another pair request already claims is accepted when its expiry is later), and the recently-removed cache (8,500 entries for a minute) is bounded by count, not bytes. Pinned by `un_orphaned_messages_keep_one_per_type_and_session` (`crates/dcroxide-mixing/tests/review_mixpool.rs`). |
| Mixpool scheduled expiry | `ExpireMessagesInBackground` latches the *first* height armed (`if p.expireHeight == 0`, `mixpool.go:426-428`) and its goroutine sleeps to the second epoch boundary after the call (`waitForExpiry`, `:439-444`), so an expired pair request survives at least one full epoch after the block that expired it | the latch keeps the *newest* height and the daemon's epoch ticker drains it at the next boundary (`run_epoch_tick`, `crates/dcroxide-node/src/mixnode.rs`) | One shared ticker can coalesce several block connects into one pass, and a first-wins latch would then expire against a stale height; tracking the newest height only removes pair requests already unusable at the tip. Consequence: an expired pair request and the sessions built on it can be dropped up to one epoch sooner than dcrd drops them, so for that epoch the port may stop serving messages dcrd peers still relay. dcrd's own mix client expires its pool on every epoch tick with the latest height (`mixing/mixclient/client.go:917`, `:1070-1071`), so wallets stop pairing them at the same boundary. Pinned by `expiry_latch_tracks_the_newest_height` (`crates/dcroxide-mixing/tests/mixpool_expiry.rs`). |

### Known remaining gaps

Tracked rather than hidden; see [SECURITY.md](SECURITY.md).

Two kinds of entry live here and they call for opposite actions, so each now
sits under its own heading. Reading the whole section as a work list sent
three separate investigations at settled entries -- the SOCKS isolation
credentials, `--maxsameip`, and nearly the mempool's modulo reductions -- each
of which ended in "this already matches dcrd, leave it" only after a full dig.
An entry moves between the two subsections when the code moves, not when the
prose is rewritten.

#### Accepted: the port matches dcrd here

Closing one of these would introduce a divergence, which this project treats as
a security bug in its own right (SECURITY.md, *Scope*). They are recorded
because the behaviour is worth knowing about -- an unbounded structure, a
resource cost, a deliberate departure that is settled -- not because anyone
should act on them. Where a bullet reads like a complaint, the complaint is
against upstream and the port is faithful.

- **Panic policy: abort, deliberately unlike dcrd.** `sync.Mutex` does not
  poison and dcrd recovers per goroutine, so a panicking goroutine there
  costs one request. Rust's mutexes poison, so one thread's panic takes
  down every other consumer of that lock — and the RPC layer's
  `catch_unwind` then kept the process alive answering canned errors, so
  the node wedged while looking healthy and `Restart=on-failure` never
  fired. `[profile.release]` now sets `panic = "abort"`: a consensus
  daemon cannot reason about state a panic left half-mutated, so a clean
  restart beats continuing. Dev and test builds keep unwinding (the
  harness needs it, and several suites use `#[should_panic]`), which also
  makes the remaining `catch_unwind` guards inert in release. Pinned by
  `crates/dcroxide-node/tests/panic_policy.rs`, which fails if the
  setting is dropped. Operators must run under a supervisor.

- **`dbCache` overlay layering.** dcrd's `dbCache` is an immutable treap, so a
  reader's snapshot is O(1) and shares structure with the writer. The port
  reaches the same property with a layered overlay rather than a treap: an
  ordered stack of immutable `BTreeMap` layers, newest first, where a snapshot
  clones only the (one-to-three-deep) list of layer handles. A commit writes
  into the newest layer in place while the cache is its only owner; a reader
  holding a snapshot freezes that layer and the commit seals a fresh one above
  it, so no transaction ever copies the overlay to make progress. Layers merge
  back geometrically (the newest merges down once it reaches half the entry
  count below it), which keeps the lookup path — every layer is probed before
  it can answer "not cached" — shallow rather than pinned at the eight-layer
  ceiling. Because a sealed layer cannot be mutated, a key rewritten under a
  reader is held once per layer, so the size accounting counts *retained*
  entries, not the overlay's logical size. Each entry is counted at dcrd's
  treap node size: `nodeFieldsSize` (72) plus its key and value bytes, for
  tombstones as for live values. For a single layer that is exactly the figure
  dcrd's `needsFlush` gates the 100 MiB ceiling on. The 72 also tracks this
  port's memory: a `BTreeMap` overlay entry holds about 74 bytes beyond its
  key and value. Counting a rewritten key once (as a single treap legitimately
  does) put the figure ~5× under the bytes actually held. Counting key and
  value bytes without the per-entry term put small-row overlays 2–4× under (an
  `existsaddridx` row is a 25-byte key and an empty value). Pinned by
  `dbcache.rs`'s own suite, which sizes entries as treap nodes
  (`entries_are_sized_as_dcrds_treap_nodes`), asserts the running total
  against what the layers hold, exercises the flush against a genuinely
  three-deep stack, and fails if the merge rule is deleted. One boundary: a
  reader's snapshot can name a layer the stack has already merged away, and
  those bytes stay allocated until the reader drops — uncounted, because they
  belong to a transaction's lifetime rather than the overlay's, which is where
  dcrd's accounting draws the same line.

- **One redb table where dcrd has two databases, at 1.738x its own payload
  against goleveldb's 1.081x.** dcrd keeps chain metadata in a goleveldb at
  `<datadir>/blocks_ffldb/metadata` and the utxo set in a second goleveldb at
  `<datadir>/utxodb`; the port keeps the whole ffldb keyspace, utxo set
  included, in one redb table per accepted
  [ADR-0004](docs/adr/0004-storage-backend.md). At the mainnet tip that costs
  disk: 32.06 GiB against dcrd's 23.73 GiB, measured 2026-07 under redb 2.6.3,
  and 33.58 against 23.69 when the pair was re-measured 2026-08-15 under
  4.1.0 with composition verified on both sides.
  The block bytes are consensus data and match to within a mebibyte
  (17.579 GiB against 17.580), so the whole 8.33 GiB gap is metadata — one
  14.483 GiB `metadata.redb` against dcrd's 6.045 + 0.108 GiB. None of it is
  data dcroxide keeps and dcrd does not: on matched composition the payload is
  equal across fifteen buckets to a 54-byte residual on 6.06 GB. Compression
  is not the reason either: dcrd opens its chain databases with
  `opt.NoCompression` (`database/ffldb/db.go:2095`,
  `internal/blockchain/utxobackend.go:365`), so both sides store raw bytes
  and the gap is per-key structural overhead. Over 5.65 GiB of payload in
  76,302,003 rows redb's live B-tree is 9.79 GiB on the 2026-07 store and
  9.82 on the matched-composition one — 0.69 GiB of per-pair and
  branch overhead plus 3.44 GiB of intra-page slack at 64.86% fill — which is
  the 1.738x above, 1.726x on dcrd's own write schedule. The 4.69 GiB of
  allocated-but-free pages is reused working space and is withdrawn as a
  comparison metric: it swings several-fold between runs of the same
  configuration while the live tree stays pinned. That is also why the live
  tree is the figure to quote and the whole file is not — matched
  composition has produced 14.00, 14.48 and 16.00 GiB apparent, all of them
  under redb 2.6.3 and none re-measured since the 4.1.0 upgrade. Compaction is
  not a way out: its yield varies by more than an order of magnitude
  between stores of the same chain (33x on apparent size, 20x on the
  consumed bytes this file no longer quotes)
  (0.12 GiB in 598.5 s on one, 3.950 GiB apparent in 137.9 s on another)
  because it relocates pages forward and truncates rather than repacking —
  fill is 0.646166 before and after, to the digit — so it is not an operator
  knob. A sorted copy-out rebuild shrinks the file only by clearing free
  pages, and packs *worse* doing it: 58.29% fill, live tree 10.92 GiB against
  9.79. The 2.48 GiB it was once credited with was recorded in consumed bytes,
  which the 2026-08-12 correction rejects. The throughput cost is a separate
  engine property: a copy-on-write B-tree does no background work, so commit
  cost tracks tree size, where goleveldb's LSM commit is O(dirty) and defers
  the rest to background compaction. Syncing mainnet from genesis the port
  spent 80.1% and 82.4% of wall time (two runs) inside progress stalls over
  20 s, while dcrd stalled 0 times in 754 windows. The node is flush-bound
  under fast ingest, which is the leading explanation for the initial block
  download gap — re-measured 2026-08-15 at **1.29x**, superseding the ~2.2x
  quoted through 2026-07. A task-state decomposition the same day measured the
  mechanism: the port drives **11.7x** the kernel-side storage work dcrd does
  (mostly dm-crypt writeback) and blocks **30x more per GiB written**, while
  dcrd writes 1.16x more bytes at 1.74x the rate — so it is the write shape,
  not the volume, and most of the cost is paid in kernel threads rather than
  the port's own. Its share of sync wall time is measured too: the port is
  **fully stalled — zero runnable threads — for 34.6% of block-sync wall
  time** against dcrd's 0.9%, rising to 50.9% above block 900,000 as the tree
  grows, and removing all of it would bring both implementations to the same
  ~346 blk/s. That supersedes the 18% derived from an identical-engine
  `--addrindex` replay (863 s of 4,767), which validates every block where the
  daemon skips ~93% under assume-valid. Attributed 2026-08-16 by pairing the
  sampler with dcroxide's own flush observer: **90–98% of the stalled time is
  inside a metadata-flush window**, and the stall is **48% of wall** once the
  sampler's own starvation is corrected (34.6% was a count-weighting
  artifact). The counterfactual is not quotable as a prize — removing only the
  commit stall projects a rate faster than dcrd's, so the model is too
  generous. The
  free pages are copy-on-write churn and nothing more: an earlier note here
  suspected that `Database::begin` taking a redb read transaction for the life
  of every ffldb transaction (`lib.rs:1202`) pinned them, since redb will not
  reclaim a freed page past the oldest live read. Measured, that shape costs
  nothing — a reader opened before a write and still live at its commit
  produces a byte-identical file to no reader at all, because the next
  commit's reader is younger. A control run with no reader in the process
  reached a larger free fraction than the real file does. Only a reader
  spanning *many* commits hurts, and none exists: no type in the workspace
  stores a `Transaction`, so every read transaction's lifetime is bounded by
  one call frame. ADR-0004's 2026-07-26 amendment carries the full
  measurement record: both data directories side by side, the four-way
  throughput matrix, the per-bucket payload breakdown, the compaction and
  rebuild results, and the redb 2.6.3 mechanisms that bound the fill.

- **The index readers never take the index mutex.** dcrd's index readers
  take no index-wide lock. `ExistsAddress`/`ExistsAddresses` open a database
  view directly and take `unconfirmedLock` only for the mempool overlay.
  `AddUnconfirmedTx` takes `unconfirmedLock` alone. `Entry`/`Tip`/`Name`
  read the database or return a constant
  (`internal/blockchain/indexers/existsaddrindex.go` 301-364, 571-575;
  `txindex.go`). The port shares each index behind a mutex because the
  daemon hands it to several threads. Reproducing dcrd's structure naively
  therefore coupled every reader to the index writer, in both directions. In
  one direction, a caller-sized `existsaddresses` (limited-credential, up to
  the 8 MiB body cap) held the mutex across its reads. The writer claims the
  database's writer semaphore before it waits on that mutex, so one such
  call stalled block connection for its whole duration. In the other
  direction, the writer holds the mutex through its wait for the database
  writer and a whole block's index work. The mempool's `add_unconfirmed_tx`
  hook (which runs under the pool mutex), every `existsaddress` and every
  `getrawtransaction` waited for it, as long as any metadata or UTXO flush
  elsewhere. The overlay now sits behind its own `RwLock`, and every reader
  holds a handle the daemon takes once when it wires the index up. Lookups
  and the tip go through `ExistsAddrQuery`, the mempool hook through
  `ExistsAddrUnconfirmed`, and `getrawtransaction` through `TxIndexQuery`;
  the seams' `name` is a constant. This is dcrd's locking shape reached
  through different machinery. The subscriber takes the index guard before
  opening its write transaction rather than after, so nothing waits on
  another lock while holding the writer semaphore. It also reads the chain
  tip for its sync check before retaking the guard, so the index mutex is
  never held across a chain-lock wait. Pinned by
  `dcroxide-node/tests/b6_indexlock.rs`,
  `dcroxide-node/tests/review_index_locks.rs` and
  `the_sync_check_reads_the_chain_tip_without_the_index_lock` in
  `dcroxide-indexers/src/subscriber.rs`.

- **The websocket notification pipeline is unbounded, as dcrd's is.** Two
  accumulators sit between a chain event and a client's socket: the manager's
  `std::sync::mpsc::channel()` (`websocket.rs:298`) and the per-client
  `OutboundQueue`, plus three `Vec` staging queues in the chain callback
  (`chainntfns.rs:103`, `:106`, `:110`). The callback queues everything except
  the early checked-block peer relay, because it runs inside the chain's
  critical section. The per-client half is two deques rather
  than the one this entry used to describe, because dcrd's is two: `items` is
  `sendChan`, carrying every reply and at most one notification, and
  `held_notifications` is `pendingNtfns`, holding the rest behind a `waiting`
  flag (`websocket.rs:106`, `:109`, `:113`). dcrd is unbounded in the same two
  places — the manager's `q []any` slice behind `queueHandler`'s select loop and
  each client's `pendingNtfns [][]byte` behind `notificationQueueHandler` — with
  no drop policy, no slow-client disconnect, and no write deadline on the
  data-frame write. So a client that stops reading grows node memory in both
  implementations; the cost of a slow reader is borne by the node, and no
  notification is ever dropped or reordered. Capping it with drops or a
  slowness disconnect was considered and rejected: it would diverge from dcrd
  *and* punish an honest client on a slow link, which is the failure mode this
  campaign kept finding. dcrd does know the pattern — its mining-template feed is
  a bounded channel with an explicit drop policy — and deliberately does not
  apply it here.

  Where the port is **stronger** than dcrd: a stalled client cannot block writes
  to other clients — each connection has its own writer thread and its own
  stream mutex, so no lock is shared across clients — and cannot block the
  chain-notification callback, so it cannot stall block processing, where dcrd's
  single fan-out goroutine head-of-line blocks every client behind one stalled
  TCP peer. That writer thread, and the `pendingNtfns` throttle above, landed
  after this entry was first written (`a6b8650`, `96e9eb5`, `4609e44`); its
  citations pointed at unrelated code until this commit, and it described the
  port as writing notifications inline from the delivery thread, which it no
  longer does. The port used to be **weaker** in one respect, since corrected: the
  server was shared as a single `Arc<Mutex<Server<NodeRpcChain>>>` held for the
  whole of any one client's request, so a multi-thousand-block `rescan`
  serialized every other client's request *and* notification construction. dcrd
  has no such lock — `wsClient.serviceRequest`
  (`internal/rpcserver/rpcwebsocket.go`) calls the handler directly, and
  `rpcserver.Server` carries only three fine-grained mutexes, each guarding a
  single field (`hmacMu` at `rpcserver.go:5230`, `statusLock` at `:5235`,
  `blake256HaserMu` at `:5244`). The port now matches that shape: every handler
  seam takes `&self`, the server is shared as a plain `Arc<Server<…>>`, and the
  only interior locks guard one field each — `work_state` (dcrd's `workState`
  embeds `sync.Mutex`, `rpcserver.go:585`, and `handleGetWorkRequest` takes it in
  four short sections at `:3890`, `:3919`, `:3959`, and `:3987`, holding it
  across neither the unbounded template wait nor the chain query that follows;
  the port's `handle_get_work_request` scopes it the same way), `subsidy_cache`
  (dcrd's `SubsidyCache` guards its own state with an internal `sync.RWMutex`,
  `blockchain/standalone/subsidy.go:100`, so the lock simply moved to the field
  holding it), and the help cacher's `method_help`/`usage` (dcrd's `helpCacher`
  embeds one `sync.Mutex` covering both fields, `rpcserverhelp.go:1058`, and
  holds it across generation in both `RPCMethodHelp` and `RPCUsage`; the port
  gives each cache its own lock. The `usage` lock is held across generation as
  dcrd holds its mutex across `RPCUsage` (`:1094-1127`), because the usage text
  depends on the websocket flag: the first caller to take the lock fixes the
  variant both transports receive, racing callers included (QK-0005). The
  `method_help` lock is released across generation, so two callers racing a
  cold entry can both generate; that text depends only on the method name, so
  they agree, and the second simply overwrites).

  The per-client state took one round of this too. dcrd's `wsClient` embeds a
  single mutex it takes a field at a time (`rpcwebsocket.go:1331`, `:303`,
  `:2333-2340`, `:2354-2356`); the port had been holding that lock across the
  whole request. The delivery thread locks each target client's state to build a
  notification, and `MempoolTx` targets every connected client, so one client's
  long call — a rescan, a `generate`, a `getwork` template wait — stalled fan-out
  to all of them. The handlers take it where they use it now, and `rescan` takes
  it per block rather than across the scan, which is where dcrd takes its own.
  Three check-then-act pairs survive that split because the client's own serving
  thread is the sole writer of the filter slot and the delivery thread only ever
  adds outpoints to an existing filter; the two that could be split without harm
  are kept under one guard anyway, so a later reader is not invited to assume
  otherwise. Pinned by `a_parked_rescan_does_not_hold_the_client_lock`, which
  measures whether the lock is free *while* a scan is parked — an earlier version
  asserted only that it came free eventually, and passed against the bug.

  Two pieces of serialization the coarse lock had been supplying by accident had
  to be put back explicitly, because dcrd has its own mechanism for each and the
  port had been leaning on the wrapper instead. First, `getwork`: dcrd serializes
  whole invocations — requests *and* submissions — through a single-item
  semaphore (`workState.workSem`, `rpcserver.go:571`, `makeSemaphore(1)` at
  `:595`, acquired `:4171`, released `:4175`), which the per-field `work_state`
  lock does not replace, since the request path deliberately releases that lock
  across the template wait. Without it two invocations can interleave their four
  critical sections and one can clear `wait_for_updated_template` after another
  re-armed it for a newer tip, leaving the next caller a template built on the
  stale parent. The port takes a `work_sem` at the same point. It is a
  condvar semaphore so a queued caller can give up when cancelled (see the
  getwork cancellation entry below for how far that reaches). Its release
  hands the permit straight to the longest-queued waiter that is still live,
  as the channel receive completes the sender at the head of the send queue
  (`rpcwebsocket.go:60-67`) and skips senders whose `select` already took
  `ctx.Done()`. Callers are therefore served in arrival order, and one that
  arrives as the permit comes free cannot overtake them. Pinned by
  `waiters_are_served_in_arrival_order` and
  `the_handoff_passes_over_a_cancelled_waiter` in `worksem.rs`.
  Second, the CPU miner: dcrd's `CPUMiner` embeds one `sync.Mutex` and holds it
  across the *whole* check-and-set in both `GenerateNBlocks`
  (`cpuminer.go:833-856`) and `SetNumWorkers` (`:697-706`), because the two
  guards are cross-checks — `generate` rejects on `normalMining`, `setgenerate`
  ignores on `discreteMining`. Splitting that pair across two atomics turns both
  into check-then-act, and two concurrent RPC threads can each pass; the port
  keeps them as one `MiningMode` struct behind one lock.

- **The RPC read is sliced for the same reason the peer read is.** dcrd's
  handlers are goroutines, so closing a connection makes the handler's `Read`
  return on any platform; the port's handlers are OS threads, and the
  pre-authentication pool's eviction depends on a victim's handler noticing the
  socket shut down under it and releasing its slot. A receive armed with the
  whole remaining budget does not come back when another thread shuts the socket
  down mid-flight under Winsock, so a victim slept out the ten-second
  authentication timeout, its entry stayed counted, the pool pinned at its
  ceiling, and every later arrival — an operator's `dcrctl` included — was
  refused with no reply. The receives are now issued in one-second slices, which
  is enough: only the receive already in flight survives the shutdown, and the
  next one fails. The ceiling still caps the pool, deliberately — an evicted
  entry holds its handler thread until that handler returns, so admitting past
  the ceiling on the strength of an eviction would let the thread count climb
  without bound, which is the exhaustion the budget exists to prevent. As on the
  peer side, a slice elapsing is not a timeout: `WouldBlock`/`TimedOut`
  continues, because otherwise any client that paused mid-request would be cut
  off. Pinned by `a_client_that_pauses_mid_request_is_still_served`, verified to
  fail when that arm is removed.

- **The port answers to its own name throughout.** Data directory
  `~/.dcroxide` (`$LOCALAPPDATA/Dcroxide`, `~/Library/Application
  Support/Dcroxide`), config file `dcroxide.conf`, and environment defaults
  `DCROXIDE_APPDATA` / `DCROXIDE_ALT_DNSNAMES` where dcrd uses `.dcrd`,
  `dcrd.conf`, and `DCRD_*`. The point is running beside a dcrd without
  either instance reaching into the other's state: a `DCRD_APPDATA`
  exported for dcrd would otherwise redirect this node's entire data
  directory at dcrd's, and a shared data directory is the one thing the
  exclusive database lock exists to prevent. Deliberately no fallback to
  the `DCRD_*` variables — honouring them would reintroduce exactly that
  collision. Everything else about the configuration surface is dcrd's,
  option names and error strings included, and the vectors stay faithful
  dumps of dcrd's own output: the comparisons normalize the port's names
  back to dcrd's (`tests/common/mod.rs`'s `as_dcrd_names`) rather than
  editing what dcrd produced. One consequence worth naming, since it
  cannot be normalized away: `DCROXIDE_APPDATA` is four characters longer
  than `DCRD_APPDATA`, which pushes the `[$VAR]` annotation onto its own
  line under go-flags' wrapping, so the *rendered* help differs from
  dcrd's by that reflow. The layout algorithm is still pinned byte for
  byte, by rendering the vector comparison with dcrd's names; what the
  production help advertises is pinned separately, so help that named a
  variable the daemon ignores would fail the suite.

- **A half-present RPC cert pair is not regenerated.** dcrd guards `genCertPair`
  with `!keyFileExists && !certFileExists` (`server.go` 3846), so with one file
  present it generates nothing and startup fails on the missing one. The port
  regenerated whenever either file was missing, which silently destroyed a
  private key an operator may have provisioned — on a path where nothing in the
  request said "replace my key". It now matches dcrd: both-absent to generate,
  otherwise an error naming the missing half with the surviving file untouched.
  Relatedly, neither dcrd nor the port inspects the mode of a file it did not
  create — dcrd contains no `Chmod`, `FileMode`, or `Mode()` call anywhere — so a
  key left group-readable by an earlier install stays that way and is the
  operator's to fix; tightening it would diverge for no gain against a threat
  model in which a local user who can read the datadir can read the key
  regardless. The certificate is written with an explicit 0644 (dcrd's mode)
  rather than `std::fs::write`'s 0666, which under a permissive umask would
  leave it group- and world-writable — an integrity question, since anyone able
  to write it can swap the identity the node serves. Pinned by
  `a_half_present_pair_is_not_regenerated_over_the_surviving_file`
  (`rpclisten.rs`).

- **`--maxsameip` keys on the exact IP**, so an IPv6 /64 can occupy every
  connection slot. Exact dcrd parity (`connmanager.go` 685); the rate limiter
  groups by /64 but the per-host permit does not. Pinned by the `tsip|` rows of
  `srvtargetout_vectors.rs`, which replay dcrd's own arithmetic for the flag.

- **`banned_hosts` grows without bound**, reclaimed only lazily on reconnect
  from that exact host. Exact dcrd parity (`server.go` 2201). The analogous
  structure at `ratelimiter.rs` is bounded at `MAX_GROUP_LIMITERS`; the same
  pattern applies here.

- **The median time source's set of seen sources grows without bound.** Each
  handshaken peer's address, port included, is remembered for the life of the
  process so that it contributes one clock sample at most, exactly as dcrd's
  `medianTime.knownIDs` does (`internal/blockchain/mediantime.go`). An inbound
  peer that reconnects from new ports adds an entry per connection. Exact dcrd
  parity; the same shape as `banned_hosts` above.

- **`gettxoutsetinfo`'s forced flush takes the chain lock where dcrd takes only
  its cache lock — deliberately, and this one is settled rather than pending.**
  The expensive half already matches: the walk of the UTXO set runs with no
  chain lock held, as dcrd's `backend.FetchStats()` (`utxobackend.go:529`) runs
  with neither `chainLock` nor `cacheLock`. Only the bounded forced flush ahead
  of it still holds the whole chain, where dcrd confines it to `cacheLock`
  (`utxocache.go:794`).

  Moving the port's flush to a cache-only lock would be a regression, not a
  fix. Both implementations connect a block as commit → flush → publish tip
  (dcrd `chain.go:728`/`:739`/`:746`; the port `process.rs:2654`/`:2656`/
  `:2659`), and dcrd reads `bestChain.Tip()` in `FetchUtxoStats`
  (`utxocache.go:1084`) with no `chainLock`. A stats call landing between the
  flush and the tip publication therefore force-flushes against the previous
  tip and leaves a `lastFlushHash` one block behind a backend that already
  holds the newer block. Neither implementation absorbs a re-applied block on
  the catch-up replay — dcrd asserts `"view missing input"`
  (`utxoviewpoint.go:292`, reached from `utxocache.go:1015`) and the port
  raises the same guard as `Corrupt` — so the node fails to open. In the port
  the flush, the tip read and the tip publication are all `&mut Chain` and so
  all under one lock, which closes that window. Splitting the flush out would
  open it. The port is intentionally stronger than upstream here.

- **Peer teardown raises a flag where dcrd closes the connection.** dcrd ends a
  connection with `Peer.Disconnect`, which closes the `net.Conn` *and* CASes
  `p.disconnect` / closes `p.quit` in one method under one mutex, so every
  server-side teardown gets both halves and its reader unwinds immediately on
  every platform. The port cannot close a descriptor out from under a reading
  thread without `unsafe`, and `shutdown` does not reliably cut a pending
  `recv` under Winsock, so each connection carries a `transport::Cancel` the
  reader polls in `READ_POLL_INTERVAL` (1 s) slices. That much was already
  true; what was divergent was that the port wrote dcrd's one method out as two
  statements at each site, and three of them performed only the `shutdown`
  half. `transport::Teardown` now owns the socket handle and the flag together
  and exposes one `disconnect()`, and it has replaced the bare `TcpStream` in
  every field and parameter on a disconnect path, so a site with a lone socket
  to shut down no longer exists. The flag is minted at the accept and at the
  dial rather than inside `serve_peer`, so every holder shares one. Pinned at
  each of the six sites plus the clone-sharing invariant beneath them --
  `a_cloned_teardown_shares_one_flag_and_disconnect_does_both_halves` and
  `separately_minted_teardowns_do_not_share_a_flag`, both unit tests in
  `transport.rs` rather than in `tests/`. The residual cost is dcrd's own shape
  reproduced: one wake-up per second per idle
  connection (~125 at the default `--maxpeers`), against dcrd's blocking read
  with no poll at all.

- **External-address selection is ported and wired.** The subsystem follows the
  parity pin -- `ExternalAddrCandidate`, `ExternalAddrCandidateCache` over
  `dcroxide_containers::lru::Map`, `best_candidate`,
  `resolve_external_address`, `consider_reported_addr_outbound` and
  `consider_reported_addr` mirror `036b7090`'s `server.go:2373-2614` -- and it
  now has a live caller. `ServerPeerHandler` stores the address a peer reports
  for the connection from its version message (dcrd
  `serverPeer.reportedLocalAddr`, `server.go:1038`), `ServerContext` holds one
  candidate cache for the process behind its own mutex (dcrd's server field,
  `server.go:424-427`), and `on_connected` calls `consider_reported_addr` once,
  unconditionally, after the outbound-only advertise block and before the
  peer-state insert, which is where dcrd calls it (`server.go:2670`). The
  advertise block itself was already present, in `on_connected` rather than in
  `handle_add_peer` -- a placement difference, not a missing behaviour, and
  this entry previously claimed the port did not have it at all.
  Two gates read deliberately different sources, as upstream does: the
  advertise block reads the cfg `--simnet`/`--regnet` flags while the discovery
  gate one level down reads the active chain params name. Pinned end to end by
  `crates/dcroxide-node/tests/srvextaddr_wired.rs`, which drives a real
  handshake over a loopback socket through `run_peer_connection`, and row for
  row against the pin by `srvextaddr_vectors.rs`.

  `handle_add_peer` itself still has no daemon caller: the peer-state maps it
  maintains are the port's `SyncPeers` registry, populated elsewhere, so what
  remains of it is admission bookkeeping the runtime already does its own way.
  That is a separate question from external-address discovery, which is now
  live.
- **RPC TLS material reloads when the files change.** dcrd hangs a
  `GetConfigForClient` callback off the listener's `tls.Config`
  (`makeReloadableTLSConfig`, `server.go:3796-3822`, wired at `:3860`), so an
  arriving connection re-stats `rpc.cert`, `rpc.key` and, under
  `--authtype=clientcert`, the `--clientcafile` bundle -- at most once every
  five seconds -- and swaps in a freshly parsed config when any of them
  changed. The port now has the same subsystem: `ReloadableTlsConfig` in
  `node/src/rpcrun.rs` mirrors `reloadableTLSConfig`, `WatchedFile` mirrors
  `watchedFile`, and `config_for_client` stands exactly where dcrd's callback
  does, because the port already built a `rustls::ServerConnection` per
  accepted connection. A rotated certificate is served without a restart, and
  under `--authtype=clientcert` a removed issuer stops being trusted on the
  first connection after the interval elapses -- which matters because editing
  that bundle is the whole revocation mechanism, neither implementation
  consulting a CRL or OCSP.

  Three of dcrd's warts come across deliberately. `needsReload`'s `||` chain
  short-circuits, so a cert and key rotated together reload twice -- once when
  the cert is noticed, then again on the next check when the key finally
  stats against details that went stale -- and log twice; Rust's `||`
  short-circuits identically, so a direct translation inherits it, and
  statting all three would be tidier and would not be dcrd. A file that no
  longer exists reports changed without refreshing its remembered details, so
  a deleted certificate retries on every connection rather than latching. And
  the first check is a full interval out from startup, so a file rewritten in
  the first five seconds of uptime is not noticed until then.

  A failed reload keeps the working configuration and warns only when the
  error differs from the previous one, so replacing the files with malformed
  data or deleting them cannot take the RPC server down, and a repair is
  picked up on the next check.

  Resumption is no longer carried across a reload, because there is none to
  carry: the port disables stateful resumption outright, for reasons that
  belong to the separate entry below rather than to reloading. An earlier
  version of this entry argued for carrying the session cache and ticketer
  over, on the grounds that dcrd's resumption survives a rotation -- Go
  resolves ticket keys with `originalConfig.ticketKeys(configForClient)`
  (`handshake_server.go:177`), which falls through to the long-lived outer
  config's automatically rotated keys when the reloaded one sets none
  (`common.go:1095-1124`). That reasoning was sound and is now moot.

  One bounded difference remains. dcrd's callback runs after the ClientHello,
  so only a peer that actually speaks TLS advances the five-second clock,
  while the port chooses the configuration as the connection is accepted and
  so lets a bare TCP connection consume a check. Under port-scanner noise a
  rotation can therefore be served up to one interval later than dcrd would
  serve it -- never more, since the interval bounds both. Closing it means
  driving the accept path through `rustls::Acceptor` to reach the real
  ClientHello, which is materially more machinery on the hot path than the
  bound justifies.

  Upstream since `488b8163` (2023-07-10), first carried by a dcrd release in
  2.0.0.
- **`generate 0` cancels an in-flight discrete mining call.** dcrd keeps a
  `generateCancelFn` while discrete mining is active and invokes it on the
  `n == 0` path (`internal/mining/cpuminer/cpuminer.go:159-163`, `:845-851`),
  so a concurrent `generate 0` stops the running solve, returns nothing
  itself, and leaves the cancelled caller with `ErrCancelDiscreteMining`.
  `MiningMode` now carries the same handle -- an `Arc<AtomicBool>` rather than
  a `context.CancelFunc`, since what it cancels is a wait on a channel and it
  is set from whichever connection ran `generate 0` while being read on the
  mining thread. It is published under the same lock that claims discrete
  mining and dropped by `DiscreteMiningGuard` alongside the flag, so a
  `generate 0` can neither cancel a call that has not started nor one that
  has already returned.

  The distinction between the two cancellations is the part worth checking.
  dcrd returns `ErrCancelDiscreteMining` for a `generate 0` *and* for a client
  that hung up, then tells them apart in `handleGenerate` by asking the
  request context first (`rpcserver.go:1850-1856`): a dead connection is
  `rpcConnectionClosedError`, everything else is `rpcCancelError`. The port
  carries that across in `GenerateFailure`'s two flags and checks them in
  dcrd's order, so `handle_generate`'s arms select the same way. This also
  retires the note that `is_cancel_discrete` had no reachable daemon producer
  and existed only for the vectors: it has one now.

  Two details came out of the port. The wait had to learn a second
  cancellation source, since the thread-local installed for a request cannot
  be reached from the connection running `generate 0`; `recv_with_timeout_until`
  takes the shared flag as an argument and defaults to ignoring it, so no test
  double changed. And the cancellation message is dcrd's
  `"discrete mining process canceled"` verbatim, because `rpcCancelError`
  formats it into the reply -- it is RPC surface, not an internal label. The
  port previously carried a paraphrase there, harmless only because no
  reachable path reached that arm.

  The cancellation reaches the solve worker as well as the loop, because
  dcrd's does: `solveCtx, cancel := context.WithCancel(genCtx)` makes the
  worker's context a child of the generate context, so cancelling the latter
  stops the former at once, and dcrd checks it both inside the hash loop and
  again after a solution is found, to suppress one discovered between the
  signal and the stop. The port passes the same flag into `SolveJob` and
  reads it at both points, alongside the per-solve flag that was already
  there. Without it the worker would keep hashing until the loop noticed and
  stopped it, and could submit in that window a block dcrd would have
  suppressed.

  That last half has no automated control, which is worth stating rather
  than implying otherwise. The window is bounded by one
  `CANCEL_POLL_INTERVAL`, and measured on this harness no block lands inside
  it either way -- solving a regnet template takes longer than 50ms -- so an
  assertion on chain height would pass with the guard removed and prove
  nothing. It is verified by construction against dcrd's two checks instead.

  What still differs is timing, bounded and small: dcrd's `select` wakes at
  once on `genCtx.Done()` while the port notices within one
  `CANCEL_POLL_INTERVAL` (50ms) of the template wait it is sitting in.

- **dcrd `New`'s version 3 test network cleanup pass is not ported, because it
  cannot fire.** At startup on testnet3, dcrd walks every chain tip. It
  invalidates, with notifications suppressed, any tip whose ancestor at height
  962928 (`testNet3MaxDiffActivationHeight`) is not `block962928Hash`
  (`chain.go:2498-2528`). This cleans up databases that stored the pre-reset
  branch before the maximum-difficulty checkpoint was enforced. No dcroxide
  database can hold such a branch. The only runtime block index insertion, in
  `maybe_accept_block_header`, runs `check_block_header_positional` first, and
  that rejects any other header at that height with `ErrBadMaxDiffCheckpoint`
  whether or not the block is a fast add. That check (`edca020`) predates the
  port's first persisted chain state (`e497a38`). The omission is recorded on
  `Chain::open`'s doc comment beside the legacy migration and `upgradeDB`
  paths.

#### Open: the port does not match dcrd here

These are real divergences from the parity pin and someone should eventually
close them. Each says what upstream does, what the port does instead, and what
closing it would cost.

- **Websocket requests are still serviced on the reading thread, so one
  request blocks the next.** dcrd gives each websocket client three
  goroutines -- `inHandler`, `notificationQueueHandler` and `outHandler`
  (`rpcwebsocket.go:1998-2011`) -- and services a non-batched command on a
  fourth, acquiring a per-client semaphore of capacity `RPCMaxConcurrentReqs`
  and then spawning (`:1549-1553`); batches it services inline (`:1748`). The
  port now has two of those: a reading loop and a writer thread. Concurrent
  dispatch was evaluated separately and declined; re-evaluated once the
  writer thread existed, it is still declined, but on narrower ground than
  the earlier note gave, and two of that note's premises have since gone
  false. Replies already route through the outbound queue
  (`websocket.rs:992`), as dcrd's spawned `serviceRequest` (`:1807`) ends on
  `SendMessage` (`:1925`) and so onto `sendChan` (`:1936`) -- that cost is
  paid, and measured below. Nor is the write half unsplit: reader and writer
  share the stream as a `Mutex<WsConn<S>>` (`websocket.rs:854`).

  What is not paid is the wakeup, and that is the whole objection. dcrd's
  `outHandler` writes `c.conn` while `inHandler` is blocked reading it,
  neither taking a lock, so a reply enqueued while the reader waits goes out
  at once. The port cannot copy that, because a `rustls::StreamOwned` is one
  object serving both directions: the reader holds the shared mutex across
  `read_message` (`websocket.rs:931-940`), whose socket timeout is
  `WS_POLL_INTERVAL` (`rpcrun.rs:76`, armed at `:3703`). Since 96e9eb5
  whoever holds the stream drains the queue, and that is exactly what makes
  inline dispatch fast -- the handler runs with the lock free, so the reader
  writes its own reply microseconds later on the next pass of the loop. Move
  dispatch to a worker and the reader returns to the poll immediately,
  holding the mutex, so a reply finishing 1ms later waits out the remaining
  49. A standalone model of the loop -- shared mutex, drain and read under
  one guard, 50ms socket timeout, condvar writer -- measures 0.02ms per round
  trip inline against 51.2ms spawned, stable over three runs. That is a full
  poll interval added to every reply, spent closing a divergence that costs
  only pipelining, only for the client issuing the slow request, and only
  with admin credentials: weaker than dcrd on every reply to stop being
  weaker than dcrd on one.

  The prerequisite, if this is ever revisited, is therefore not splitting the
  stream but a readiness wait taken without the stream lock -- which under
  TLS must also account for plaintext already decrypted and sitting in
  `rustls::ServerConnection`, where the socket has nothing pending yet a
  whole message is available. Two invariants stated in comments today also
  assume one request at a time per connection and would have to move first:
  the thread-local carrying the cancellation token is sound only because
  "the transport ... processes one request at a time" (`worksem.rs:27-29`),
  and ignoring mutex poisoning on the client state is justified by its
  serving thread being "its only writer" (`rpc/websocket.rs:326`).

  The writer thread closes the largest consequence: a notification now
  reaches a client while one of that client's own requests is still running,
  which is what `outHandler` gives dcrd. Replies are queued rather than
  written inline, as dcrd queues its own through `SendMessage`
  (`:1925-1940`), so one writer drains one queue and output keeps its order.
  Measured, because the shape invites a latency regression and the first draft
  had one: thirty sequential round trips averaged 41.3ms here against 41.4ms
  before the change. Both figures were the delayed-ACK floor, not the loop.
  Each frame went out as two writes (header, then payload) on a socket with
  Nagle's algorithm on, so every payload waited for the client's delayed ACK
  of its header. Frames are now one write, as gorilla's server fast path is
  (`conn.go:775-785`). Every socket the node accepts or dials gets
